4.4 Overtime
Detail the statutory rules for overtime, including eligibility criteria and mandatory rates.
The maximum ordinary working week. Labour Code art. 161, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 11: a maximum of 8 hours per day and 42 hours per week, distributable by agreement over five or six days, always preserving the rest day and without reducing salary. Law 2101 of 2021 art. 3 sets the gradual path; the final step to 42 hours takes effect on 15 July 2026.
Period
Maximum ordinary week
15 July 2025 – 14 July 2026
44 hours
From 15 July 2026
42 hours
Where the week is distributed over five days so that the employee rests on Saturday and/or Sunday, the ordinary daily shift may be extended by up to two hours without paying supplementary work (Labour Code art. 164, confirmed by Circular 101 of 2025 §2.1).
Eligibility. Overtime is due to every employee whose hours exceed the agreed ordinary day or, absent agreement, the statutory maximum. Exclusions and special regimes:
Category
Position
Management, trust and confidential staff (empleados de dirección, confianza y manejo)
Excluded from the maximum-hours rules. Law 2466 of 2025 art. 70 repealed Labour Code art. 162 literal b) — the exclusion for intermittent-activity workers and domestic staff is gone; only the direction/trust/management exclusion of literal a) survives
Employees on all-inclusive salary
Overtime is compensated in advance by the salary itself (Labour Code art. 132 num. 2) — except vacation, which is never absorbed
Employees on 6-hour / 36-hour-week successive shift systems
No night premium and no rest-day premium apply (Labour Code arts. 161(d) and 168 num. 1)
Flexible daily shifts within 42 hours per week over up to 6 days, 4 to 9 continuous hours per day
No supplementary-work premium (Labour Code art. 161(a))
Minors 15–17
6 hours per day, 30 hours per week, daytime only
Minors over 17
8 hours per day, 40 hours per week
Piece-rate and agricultural workers
Included — Circular 101 of 2025 §§2.3–2.4 is explicit that they are not excluded
Volume limits. Labour Code art. 22 of Law 50 of 1990, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 13: "In no case may overtime hours, whether daytime or night-time, exceed two (2) hours per day and twelve (12) per week." Exceptions: private security (Law 1920 of 2018) and the health sector. Circular 101 derives the resulting maxima of ordinary plus overtime hours: 56 hours per week to 14 July 2026 and 54 hours per week from 15 July 2026.
Ministry authorisation abolished; a register imposed instead. Law 2466 of 2025 art. 12 removed the requirement for Ministry of Labour authorisation to work overtime, and replaced it with an employer duty to keep a supplementary-work register recording, as a minimum: the activity performed, the total supplementary hours, the split between day and night hours, and evidence of payment. It must be available on demand to employees and to administrative and judicial authorities. Failure to keep it, or failure to pay supplementary work, allows inspectors to order the suspension of all supplementary work for six months.
The night window moved. Labour Code art. 160, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 10: day work is 06:00 to 19:00 and night work is 19:00 to 06:00. Article 160 par. 2 delayed entry into force by six months from the 25 June 2025 sanction, so the 19:00 boundary has applied since 25 December 2025 and governs the whole of 2026. Two extra hours per day now attract the night premium compared with the pre-reform 21:00 boundary.
Rates — Labour Code art. 168 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 24, not amended by the labour reform):
Type
Premium
Note
Night work, by reason of being night work
+35% on the value of day work
Excludes the 36-hour shift system
Day overtime
+25% on the ordinary day rate

Night overtime
+75% on the ordinary day rate

Article 168 num. 4 is critical: "Each of the aforementioned premiums arises exclusively, that is to say, without being cumulated with any other." The night, day-overtime and night-overtime premiums are alternatives, not cumulative with one another. The obligatory-rest-day premium of art. 179 is a separate statutory head and is added to them.
Work on an obligatory rest day or public holiday — Labour Code art. 179, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 14. The article sets the surcharge at 100%, with a transitional paragraph phasing it in:
Period
Rest-day / holiday premium
1 July 2025 – 30 June 2026
80%
1 July 2026 – 30 June 2027
90%
From 1 July 2027
100%
An employer may voluntarily apply 100% from the outset.
The three-value structure. Circular 101 of 2025 §4 is explicit that working on an obligatory rest day generates three independent payment obligations, not one:
the paid rest day itself, at one day's ordinary salary (Labour Code art. 173) — first value;
the hours actually worked on that day — second value;
the additional premium on the hour value at 80% / 90% / 100% (art. 179) — third value.
"Payment is made for the rest day, payment is made for the hours worked, and an additional premium is paid for working on Sundays or public holidays." An engine that pays only the hours plus the premium underpays by one day.
Occasional versus habitual rest-day work. Article 179 par. 1: work on up to two obligatory rest days in a calendar month is occasional; three or more is habitual. Habitual rest-day work gives the employee the right to a compensatory rest day or to the corresponding remuneration, at the employee's election (Labour Code art. 180).
Combined 2026 percentages (art. 168 premiums applied exclusively, plus the art. 179 premium where the day is an obligatory rest day):
Hour type
Jan–Jun 2026
Jul–Dec 2026
Ordinary day hour (06:00–19:00)
100% (base)
100% (base)
Night premium (19:00–06:00)
+35%
+35%
Day overtime
+25%
+25%
Night overtime

+75%

A defect in the official guidance, recorded for traceability. Circular 101 of 2025 §5.1 contains an internal inconsistency: after correctly stating the 80/90/100 phase-in in §4, it then states "supplementary work on Sundays and public holidays with a 75% premium (Labour Code art. 179)" — the pre-reform figure. The 80/90/100 schedule in §4, which tracks the statutory text of art. 179 as substituted by Law 2466 art. 14, governs. The 75% reference in §5.1 is a residual error and must not be configured.
Explain how overtime is treated for income tax and social security calculations.
Aspect
Treatment
Salary character
Overtime and rest-day work are salary by express statutory definition — Labour Code art. 127 lists "the value of supplementary work or of overtime hours" and "the value of work on mandatory rest days"
Social-security contribution base
Fully included. No exemption, no cap other than the general 25-minimum-wage ceiling. Both employee (4% + 4% + solidarity fund) and employer (12% + 8.5% + occupational risk) contributions apply
Payroll-levy base
Fully included — Law 21 of 1982 art. 17 covers "the various constituent elements of salary"
Statutory-benefit base
Included in the service-bonus, severance-savings and interest bases. Excluded from the vacation base — Labour Code art. 192 num. 1 excludes only rest-day work and supplementary overtime hours from the vacation calculation. The night premium stays in the vacation base
Withholding tax
Fully taxable. Overtime enters the ordinary monthly taxable payments under both Procedure 1 and Procedure 2. There is no preferential or separate rate for overtime in Colombia, and no exempt overtime allowance
Threshold effects
A large overtime month can push the employee across the 10-minimum-wage payroll-levy exemption line, the 4-minimum-wage solidarity-fund trigger, or into a higher withholding band — all tested on that month's actual figures
Payment timing
With the salary of the period worked, or at the latest the following period (Labour Code art. 134 num. 2)
Electronic payroll reporting
Reported as discrete overtime and premium elements with a quantity, a start and end time, a percentage and an amount, under the annex's hour-type code table
A reporting conflict, now resolved by tax-authority doctrine. The electronic payroll Technical Annex §5.5.5 (still at version 1.0, February 2021) hard-codes the pre-reform rest-day percentages — 100 / 75 / 150 / 110 for the four rest-day hour types — while the statute requires 80% in the first half of 2026 and 90% in the second. DIAN Concepto 013840 (int. 1568) of 1 October 2025 resolves the point: the annex's published percentage "must be read in accordance with the rules in force for each specific case", and the coexistence of different premium percentages within a single month "does not constitute any obstacle to the generation, transmission and subsequent validation" of the document. Employers must consolidate all earnings of the period including premiums settled at the statutorily applicable percentage. The engine therefore reports the real statutory percentage (80% or 90%), not the annex's legacy literal.
Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 127, 134, 158, 159, 160, 161, 162, 164, 168, 169, 172, 173, 179, 180, 192 (as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 arts. 10, 11, 12, 13, 14 and 70); Law 50 of 1990 arts. 22 and 24; Law 2101 of 2021 arts. 3 and 4; Ministry of Labour Circular 101 of 22 September 2025 §§2.1, 2.3, 2.4, 3, 4, 5, 5.1; DIAN Concepto 013840 int. 1568 of 1 October 2025; Law 21 of 1982 art. 17.
4.5 Bonuses
Is there a statutory requirement to pay bonuses? If so, how are they calculated?
Yes — the statutory service bonus (*prima de servicios*) is mandatory for every private-sector employee.
Labour Code art. 306, as amended by Law 1788 of 2016 art. 1:
"The employer is obliged to pay to its employee or employees the social benefit known as the service bonus (prima de servicios), which shall correspond to 30 days of salary per year, payable in two instalments, as follows: one half by 30 June at the latest and the other half no later than the first twenty days of December. It shall be recognised for the whole half-year worked or in proportion to the time worked. PARAGRAPH. This economic benefit includes domestic-service employees, family-service drivers, day workers and farm workers…"
Element
Rule
Amount
30 days' salary per year — 15 days per semester
Instalments
Half by 30 June, half by 20 December
Proration
For the whole semester worked or proportionally to the time worked
Base
Salary plus the transport allowance. Variable pay enters at the average of the semester
Monthly accrual
8.3333% (30 ÷ 360) of the base
Legal character
Not salary — Labour Code art. 307: "The annual bonus is not salary, nor shall it be counted as a salary factor in any case"
Contribution base
Excluded
Payroll-levy base
Excluded
Coverage
Universal, including domestic workers, family drivers, day workers and farm workers
All-inclusive salary
Absorbed — the all-inclusive salary compensates statutory bonuses in advance (Labour Code art. 132 num. 2)
Set-off — Labour Code art. 308. Contractual or Christmas bonuses are set off against the statutory service bonus; where the statutory bonus is larger, the employer pays the difference. An employer already paying a 13th-month equivalent does not pay twice.
Formula and worked example.
Service bonus = (monthly salary + transport allowance) × days worked in the semester ÷ 360
Employee on COP 2,400,000 plus the transport allowance of COP 249,095, having worked the full first semester of 2026 (180 days):
(2,400,000 + 249,095) × 180 ÷ 360 = 2,649,095 × 0.5 = COP 1,324,547.50   payable by 30 June 2026
Other statutory payments that are commonly but wrongly called bonuses. Severance savings, the interest on severance savings and vacation pay are separate statutory heads with their own rules, covered in Modules 8 and 9. There is no statutory Christmas bonus, no statutory profit-sharing and no statutory performance bonus in Colombia.
Non-statutory bonuses — performance, sign-on, retention, referral, extra-statutory Christmas and vacation bonuses — are entirely contractual. They are salary if habitual, and therefore contributory, unless the parties have expressly agreed in writing that they are not salary under Labour Code art. 128, subject always to the 40% clamp.
Is there a specific tax treatment for bonuses?
Yes, and it differs sharply between the two withholding procedures. This is one of the most commonly mis-implemented rules in Colombian payroll.

Withholding Procedure 1
Withholding Procedure 2
Statutory minimum service bonus
Withheld separately, on its own read of the art. 383 table, not added to the month's salary. Tax Code art. 385: "In the case of the statutory minimum service bonus in the private sector … the 'amount to withhold' is the one shown against the bracket into which the respective bonus falls"
Folded into the ordinary monthly base; the fixed percentage is applied to the combined figure. The 13 divisor in the semi-annual calculation already prorates the bonus effect
Any excess above the statutory minimum bonus
Added to the ordinary monthly base
Ordinary monthly base
Non-statutory bonuses
Ordinary monthly base
Ordinary monthly base
Severance savings and interest
Excluded from the monthly base under both procedures — they have their own art. 206 num. 4 treatment
Same
Everything else about a bonus's tax treatment is ordinary. Bonuses are taxable employment income, enter the depuration of Tax Code art. 388, and benefit from the 25% exempt-income deduction within the combined 40% and 1,340-tax-unit annual caps. There is no preferential bonus rate, no separate annualisation method and no tax-free bonus allowance in Colombia.

Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 128, 132, 306, 307, 308 (art. 306 as amended by Law 1788 of 2016 art. 1); Tax Code arts. 206, 383, 385, 386, 388; Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30.
4.6 Wage Classification (Country-Specific)
Are there country-specific classifications for wages critical for social security or tax calculations?
Yes — four, and they are the defining feature of Colombian payroll. Getting any one of them wrong mis-states either the contribution base, the levy base, the benefit base or the withholding base.
#
Classification
Statutory source
What it decides
1
Salary vs non-salary (salario vs no constitutivo de salario)
Labour Code arts. 127 and 128
Whether a payment enters the contribution base, the payroll-levy base and the statutory-benefit base
2
The 40% non-salary clamp
Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30
How much validly non-salary pay may nonetheless be excluded from the contribution base
3
Ordinary salary vs all-inclusive salary (salario integral)
Labour Code art. 132 num. 2–3; Law 100 art. 18
Whether the contribution base and the payroll-levy base are 100% or 70% of the salary, and which statutory benefits are absorbed
4
Statutory benefits (prestaciones sociales) vs salary vs paid rest
Labour Code Titles VIII–IX; art. 128
Which payments are excluded from the contribution base, and which — like vacation — are not
Provide a detailed definition for each classification.
1. Salary vs non-salary.
Labour Code art. 127 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 14) defines salary as "everything the employee receives in money or in kind as direct consideration for the service, whatever the form or designation adopted, such as bonuses, salary supplements, habitual gratuities, the value of supplementary work or of overtime hours, the value of work on mandatory rest days, sales percentages and commissions."
Article 128 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 15) excludes: occasional and purely gratuitous sums; profit participation and solidarity-economy surpluses; amounts given "not for the employee's benefit, nor to enrich their estate, but in order to perform their duties properly" — representation expenses, means of transport, work tools; the statutory benefits of Titles VIII and IX; and "habitual or occasional benefits or allowances agreed collectively or contractually … where the parties have expressly stipulated that they do not constitute salary", such as food, housing, clothing and extra-statutory vacation, service or Christmas bonuses.
Three tests for a valid non-salary pact: it must be (a) express, (b) written, and (c) attach to a benefit or allowance, not to consideration for the service itself. A "non-salary" label on what is plainly remuneration for work will not survive a contribution audit.
2. The 40% non-salary clamp.
Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30, verbatim:
"Without prejudice to what is provided for other purposes, for the purposes related to articles 18 and 204 of Law 100 of 1993, the labour payments of private-sector employees that do not constitute salary may not exceed 40% of total remuneration."
A valid non-salary pact removes a payment from the labour-law salary base permanently — it never enters the statutory-benefit base or the overtime base. But it removes the payment from the social-security contribution base only up to 40% of total remuneration. Everything above that line is added back for health, pension and occupational risk while remaining non-salary for the benefits. Two divergent bases, permanently. The clamp bites on articles 18 and 204 of Law 100 only — it does not reach the payroll levies, which have their own base.
3. Ordinary salary vs all-inclusive salary.
Labour Code art. 132 num. 2 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 18): where the employee earns an ordinary salary above 10 minimum wages, the parties may agree in writing a salary that additionally compensates in advance the statutory benefits, premiums and allowances — night work, overtime, rest-day and holiday work, statutory and extra-statutory bonuses, severance savings and their interest, subsidies and in-kind supplies — except vacation.
Element
Rule
2026
Minimum
10 minimum wages + a benefit factor of at least 30%
13 minimum wages = COP 22,761,765
Contribution base
70% of the all-inclusive salary (Law 100 art. 18 final paragraph; Decree 1072 art. 2.2.4.3.3 for occupational risk)
At the floor: 15,933,235.50, rounded up
Payroll-levy base
Also 70% — Labour Code art. 132 num. 3: "the contributions shall be reduced by thirty percent (30%)"

Vacation
Still owed in full, 15 working days per year, on the whole all-inclusive salary

Tax
The former exemption of the benefit factor was replaced by the 25% exempt-income deduction (Tax Code art. 206 par. 2)

4. Statutory benefits vs salary vs paid rest — and why vacation is the trap.
Statutory benefits (prestaciones sociales) are employer-borne entitlements under Titles VIII and IX: the service bonus, severance savings, the interest on them, work clothing and sickness assistance. They are excluded from salary by art. 128 and therefore from the contribution base.
Vacation is not a statutory benefit. It is paid rest. The consequences are counter-intuitive and must be configured deliberately:
Vacation pay enters the contribution base — contributions continue during leave.
Vacation pay enters the payroll-levy base, because Law 21 of 1982 art. 17 expressly covers
"descansos remunerados".
Its own calculation base excludes overtime and rest-day work only (Labour Code art. 192 num. 1) — the
night premium and commissions stay in.
An all-inclusive salary does not absorb it (art. 132 num. 2 expressly excepts it), so vacation is owed
on the full all-inclusive salary while contributions run on 70%.

Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 127, 128, 129, 132, 186, 189, 192, 227, 230, 236, 249, 253, 306, 307, 308 (as amended by Law 50 of 1990 arts. 14, 15, 16, 18, Law 1788 of 2016 art. 1 and Law 2466 of 2025 arts. 21, 53); Law 100 of 1993 arts. 18 and 204; Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30; Law 21 of 1982 art. 17; Tax Code arts. 206, 387, 387-1, 388; Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.3.3; Decree 0223 of 5 March 2026.

Module 5: Social Security & Pension Systems
Purpose: To detail the rules for all mandatory social security and pension schemes.
Wage-caliber declaration (control B2). Every social-security floor, ceiling and trigger in Colombia derives from one single published figure — the statutory minimum monthly wage (SMMLV) — and from no average-wage series of any kind. Colombia's statistics office publishes average and median wage series, and the minimum-wage decree itself cites a living-wage study, consumer-price inflation, total-factor productivity, labour productivity per hour and per employed person, and gross-domestic-product growth — but none of these enters any payroll parameter. They are inputs to the annual political negotiation that fixes the minimum wage, not calibers in their own right. There is therefore no possibility of mixing calibers in Colombian payroll, and the reconciliation is trivial and exact:
Parameter
Derivation

1 × 1,750,905

4 × 1,750,905
7,003,620

10 × 1,750,905
17,509,050

13 × 1,750,905
22,761,765

28,014,480
Subsistence sub-account top band
20 × 1,750,905
35,018,100
Contribution-base ceiling
25 × 1,750,905
43,772,625
The only other calibration unit in Colombian payroll is the tax unit (UVT), COP 52,374 for 2026, and it indexes tax parameters exclusively — never a social-security parameter. The two units are never mixed. The statutory transport allowance of COP 249,095 is a fixed peso amount, not a caliber, and indexes nothing.
5.1 Contribution Types and Rates
List all mandatory social security and pension contributions for both employer and employee.
Colombia's integrated social-protection system has three insurance branches and three payroll levies, plus one employee-only solidarity surcharge. All seven are collected through a single monthly integrated contribution return.

Total standard employee burden: 8.0%, rising to 10.0% at the top of the solidarity-fund scale. Total standard employer burden: 12% + 8.5% + occupational risk + 9% levies, before the exemption.
Three of the eight are exonerated for lower-paid employees. Tax Code art. 114-1 exonerates employer health 8.5%, the apprenticeship training levy 2% and the family welfare levy 3% for employees earning less than 10 minimum wages. Pension, occupational risk and the family compensation fund 4% are never exonerated. See Module 5.3.
Newest-law check — the 2024 pension reform is NOT the operative law for FY2026 (control C2). Colombia enacted a complete replacement of its pension architecture, Law 2381 of 2024 ("establishing the Integral Social Protection System for Old Age, Invalidity and Death from Common Causes"), which replaces the two competing regimes of Law 100 of 1993 with a pillar system. It is the newest pension law on the statute book and it is the reason this guide states its pension basis explicitly. It does not govern any 2026 payroll period, and the rates in the table above are the Law 100 of 1993 rates as amended by Law 797 of 2003. The judicial history that produces that result:
Step
Instrument
Effect
Enacted
Law 2381 of 2024, art. 94
Set its own entry into force at 1 July 2025
Suspended
Constitutional Court Auto 841 of 18 June 2025
Suspended that entry into force, sparing only art. 12 and art. 76, and ordered the Chamber of Representatives to re-run the fourth plenary debate within 30 working days (minimum three hours) to cure a procedural defect
Upheld, deferred
Constitutional Court Judgment C-264 of 25 August 2026 (case D-15989)
Held art. 94 constitutional on the understanding that the upheld provisions take effect on 1 April 2027. Over 80 articles were upheld against the procedural challenge. Nine were returned to the Chamber for a cured debate: art. 11 inciso 4, art. 19 literal k, art. 14, art. 23 transitory paragraph, art. 36, art. 63 inciso 2, art. 84 num. 5, art. 92 inciso 1 and art. 93. The Court reviewed procedure only, not substance
Consequence for configuration. For every pay period from 1 January to 31 December 2026 the engine must apply Law 100 of 1993 as amended by Law 797 of 2003 — the rates, bands, floor and ceiling set out in this module. No part of Law 2381 of 2024 may be configured as live for 2026. The reform is a 2027 change-control item, and because judgment C-264 of 2026 was handed down on 25 August 2026 the parameters below must be re-verified before the April 2027 cut-over.

n/a
Employees holding 750 weeks (women) or 900 weeks (men) at entry into force stay entirely under Law 100 of 1993 (art. 75) — so the engine will need a per-employee transition flag
The employer's collection duty is unchanged in substance: the employer pays its own share and withholds the employee's share at the moment of payment (Law 2381 of 2024 art. 21, matching Law 100 of 1993 art. 22).
Are contributions managed at a national or sub-national level?
National. There is no departmental or municipal social-security contribution, no regional rate variation and no sub-national scheme. All rates, bases, floors and ceilings above apply uniformly across the country.
Three points of institutional, not rate, variation:
Point
Nature
Occupational-risk class
Varies by work centre, not by geography. A company with two work centres legitimately carries two risk classes and two rates. Where a person performs several occupations, they contribute at the highest risk class (Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.2.5.6)
Family compensation fund
Organised by department; the receiving institution code differs, the 4% rate does not
Small-business levy scales
Law 1429 of 2010 provides a graduated scale for qualifying micro and small enterprises, with a special regime for Amazonas, Guainía and Vaupés. Visible in the contribution-return rate tables as contributor classes C and D. Not applicable to standard corporate payroll
Collection is national but intermediated. The employer never transacts directly with the government for social security. Contributions are self-assessed and paid through a licensed private information operator (operador de información) which files the integrated contribution return and distributes the money to the health insurer, pension administrator, occupational-risk insurer, family compensation fund, apprenticeship service and family welfare institute. The audit counterparty is the UGPP, which cross-matches tax-authority data against filed contribution returns.
5.1.1 Contribution Basis Definition
For each mandatory contribution, provide the statutory definition of the earnings base.
Health, pension, solidarity fund and occupational risk — the contribution base (*Ingreso Base de Cotización*, IBC).
Law 100 of 1993 art. 18, as substituted by Law 797 of 2003 art. 5, verbatim highlights:
"The limit of the contribution base shall be twenty-five (25) legal monthly minimum wages in force, for public- and private-sector employees." "Contributions for employees whose remuneration is agreed under the integral-salary arrangement shall be calculated on 70% of that salary." "In no case may the contribution base be lower than one legal monthly minimum wage in force." Par. 1: where the employee has two or more employers, contributions are made proportionally to each remuneration and accumulate up to the legal ceiling; "contributions to the health system must be made on the same base".
Health uses the same base as pension — Law 100 art. 204 par. 1 — and occupational risk likewise (Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.4.3.3). The content of the base is the set of payments that are salary under Labour Code arts. 127 and 128, clamped by Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30.
Family compensation fund, apprenticeship training levy and family welfare levy — the payroll-levy base (*nómina mensual de salarios*).
Law 21 of 1982 art. 17, verbatim:
"… the monthly salary payroll means the totality of the payments made in respect of the various constituent elements of salary within the meaning of the labour law, whatever their designation, and in addition those made for statutory and collectively agreed or contractual paid rest periods." Foreign-currency payments are included, converted at the official rate on the last day of the month.
Clarify the legal definitions of any specific terms used.
Term
Definition
Contribution base (IBC)
The monthly sum of salary-constituting payments, after the 40% clamp, the 70% all-inclusive rule, the 1-minimum-wage floor and the 25-minimum-wage ceiling, rounded up to the whole peso
Payroll-levy base
The total of all salary elements plus statutory and contractual paid rest days, with no ceiling
Salary (salario)
Labour Code art. 127 — everything received in money or in kind as direct consideration for the service, whatever its name
Non-salary (no constitutivo de salario)
Labour Code art. 128 — occasional gratuities, profit participation, sums given to enable the work, the statutory benefits, and expressly-pacted benefits and allowances
All-inclusive salary (salario integral)
Labour Code art. 132 num. 2 — a written salary above 13 minimum wages compensating benefits, premiums and allowances in advance, except vacation
Statutory benefits (prestaciones sociales)
Titles VIII–IX of the Labour Code — the service bonus, severance savings, interest on severance savings, work clothing, sickness assistance
Solidarity account (subcuenta de solidaridad)
The solidarity pension fund sub-account subsidising contributions of low-income and informal workers
Subsistence account (subcuenta de subsistencia)
The solidarity pension fund sub-account funding the non-contributory old-age benefit
Public defined-benefit regime (Régimen de Prima Media, RPM)
The pay-as-you-go pension regime administered by Colpensiones
Private individual-account regime (Régimen de Ahorro Individual con Solidaridad, RAIS)
The funded regime administered by private pension administrators
Occupational risk class
I to V, assigned to the work centre by economic activity under Decree 768 of 2022
Exemption (exoneración)
The Tax Code art. 114-1 relief from employer health, apprenticeship-training and family-welfare contributions for employees below 10 minimum wages
Change event (novedad)
A within-month event reported on the contribution return that alters the base or the contributory days

Variation by age
Situation
Effect
Minors aged 15–17, lawfully employed
Full contributions on the same rates. Working hours are restricted (6 hours per day, 30 per week, daytime only), which reduces the base, not the rates
Employed old-age pensioner
No pension contribution; health, occupational risk and the levies continue. Reported with worker sub-type 01
Employee at or near retirement age
No rate variation. Retirement age (57 women / 62 men under the public defined-benefit regime) affects entitlement, not the payroll rate
High-risk activity employees (Decree-Law 2090 of 2003)
An additional employer pension contribution and a reduced retirement age. Rate not verified for 2026 — see the open item below
Variation by gender: none. There is no gender-based rate, threshold or ceiling in any of the eight contributions. Labour Code art. 143, as amended by Law 1496 of 2011, expressly forbids salary differences by gender, and the social-security statutes are gender-neutral throughout. Retirement age differs (57 for women, 62 for men in the public defined-benefit regime), which affects entitlement and the point at which the pension contribution ceases, but never the contribution rate.
Open item — high-risk pension activities. Decree-Law 2090 of 2003 establishes the high-risk activities that carry an additional employer pension contribution and a reduced retirement age, and the electronic payroll XML carries a required AltoRiesgoPension flag. The contribution-return rate table of Resolution 2388 of 2016 shows a total pension rate of 26% for a high-risk contributor (against the standard 16%), implying an additional 10 percentage points borne by the employer. The 2026 confirmation of that figure and the precise base has not been obtained from a primary regulatory source this pass. Configure only against the current implementing decree; carried in the QA report.
5.2 Calculation Logic and Edge Cases
What is the step-by-step calculation logic for all types of social security contributions?
STEP 1  Build the CONTRIBUTION BASE        1.1  Sum salary-constituting payments of the month (Labour Code arts. 127/128)             - transport and connectivity allowances EXCLUDED             - service bonus, severance savings and interest EXCLUDED             - vacation pay INCLUDED        1.2  Apply the 40% clamp: if non-salary payments > 40% of total remuneration,             add the excess back into the base   (Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30)        1.3  All-inclusive salary: base = 70% of the salary        1.4  Floor: base >= 1 minimum wage, pro-rated to contributory days        1.5  Ceiling: base <= 25 minimum wages = 43,772,625        1.6  Round the base UP to the nearest whole pesoSTEP 2  Build the PAYROLL-LEVY BASE (separate, no ceiling)        2.1  Sum all salary elements PLUS statutory and contractual paid rest days        2.2  All-inclusive salary: base = 70%        2.3  No floor, no ceiling        2.4  Round UP to the nearest whole pesoSTEP 3  EMPLOYEE deductions on the contribution base        Health         4%        Pension        4%    (0% for an employed old-age pensioner)        Solidarity fund 1% if base >= 4 minimum wages        Subsistence    0.2%-1.0% if base >= 16 minimum wages, by band        Round EACH amount UP to the nearest multiple of 100STEP 4  Evaluate the EXEMPTION (Tax Code art. 114-1)        exempt := employer_is_income_tax_filing_legal_person                  AND month_earnings < 10 x minimum wage      # STRICTLY less than        (a natural-person employer qualifies only if it employs 2 or more workers)STEP 5  EMPLOYER contributions        Pension                     12%   of contribution base            always        Health                      8.5%  of contribution base            0 if exempt        Occupational risk           class rate of contribution base       always        Family compensation fund    4%    of payroll-levy base            always        Apprenticeship training     2%    of payroll-levy base            0 if exempt        Family welfare              3%    of payroll-levy base            0 if exempt        Round EACH amount UP to the nearest multiple of 100STEP 6  Report contributory DAYS per subsystem and any change events
Are contributions based on actual or standard salary?
Actual salary, with four statutory adjustments — the floor, the ceiling, the 70% all-inclusive rule and the 40% clamp. Colombia has no notional, banded or standard contribution salary; there is no class system and no scheduled contribution wage.
Two consequences worth stating explicitly:
A variable-pay employee has a different contribution base every month, and can legitimately cross
the 4-minimum-wage solidarity trigger, the 10-minimum-wage exemption line and the 25-minimum-wage ceiling in different directions in successive months.
Where actual salary falls below the floor — a partial month, a low-paid part-time employee — the base is
raised to 1 minimum wage, pro-rated to the contributory days where a valid change event reduces them. The employee still contributes 4% + 4% of that raised base even though the salary was lower.
A special case: the apprentice. Decree 0223 of 5 March 2026 art. 2.2.6.3.3.4 par. 2 provides that "in any event the contribution base must be [at] one (1) legal monthly minimum wage in force" and that the employee-side deductions are computed on that base. So an apprentice in the classroom phase, receiving 75% of the minimum wage, nonetheless contributes on a full minimum wage.
Describe rules for eligibility, joining month, termination month, mid-period hires, mid-period terminations, and mid-period salary changes.
Eligibility. Affiliation to health, pension and occupational risk is compulsory from the first day of the employment relationship, with no waiting period, no minimum hours and no minimum earnings. Two timing rules matter:
Occupational-risk coverage starts the calendar day after affiliation (Decree 1072 of 2015
art. 2.2.4.2.5.4), and a new hire must be reported to the risk insurer no later than the business day following the start date (art. 2.2.4.3.6). An employee who starts work before affiliation is uninsured, and the employer bears the occupational-risk benefit directly.
Health and pension affiliation must be effective from the start date.
Joining month. Contributions are due for the days actually worked, with a hire change event carrying the contributory day count. The base is the actual earnings for those days, floored at the pro-rated minimum wage. The full monthly rates apply — there is no reduced first-month rate.
Termination month. Contributions are due to the termination date, with a termination change event. Termination settlement components are treated as follows:
Component
In the contribution base?
Salary to the termination date
Yes
Accrued vacation paid in cash
Yes — paid rest
Accrued service bonus
No
Accrued severance savings and interest
No
Dismissal indemnity
No
Payment in lieu of notice
No
Mid-period hire and termination in the same month. Both change events are reported; the base is the earnings of the days actually worked.
Mid-period salary change. Split the month at the effective date, prorate each rate over its own days, sum to the monthly base, and report a salary variation change event. Where an increase takes the employee across the 10-minimum-wage line for that month's total earnings, the exemption is lost for the whole month — the test is on the month, not on the segment.
Within-month change events the engine must support (contribution-return mnemonics, verified from Resolution 2388 of 2016 Technical Annex 2):

Do different payment frequencies affect contribution rates or parameters?
No. Rates, floors, ceilings and triggers are all monthly parameters, independent of how often the employee is paid. A weekly-paid employee and a monthly-paid employee on the same annual salary contribute identically.
Aspect
Effect of frequency
Rates
None
Floor and ceiling
None — both are monthly
Solidarity-fund trigger
None — tested on the monthly base
Exemption test
None — tested on the monthly earnings
Filing frequency
None — the contribution return is monthly regardless
Contributory days
Reported per subsystem for the month, whatever the number of payments
The only frequency-sensitive interaction is the five-payment weekly month discussed in Module 1.3 — which changes the amount of the monthly base, not any parameter.
Detail variations based on employee age or gender.
Age.
Age or status
Variation
15–17, lawfully employed
No rate variation. Restricted hours (6 per day, 30 per week, daytime only) reduce the base

Standard rates
Employed old-age pensioner
Pension contribution ceases entirely — neither the 4% employee nor the 12% employer share is due. Health, occupational risk and the levies continue. Worker sub-type 01
Employee in a high-risk activity
Additional employer pension contribution and reduced retirement age (Decree-Law 2090 of 2003) — see the open item in Module 5.1.1
Retirement age (57 women / 62 men, public regime)
Affects entitlement, not the rate. Reaching the age does not by itself stop the contribution; being a pensioner in active service does
Gender: no variation of any kind. No gender-differentiated rate, base, floor, ceiling or trigger exists in any of the eight contributions.
Is there a minimum number of working days required for contributions?
No. There is no minimum-days, minimum-hours or minimum-earnings qualifying condition. One day of employment generates a contribution obligation for that day, with the base floored at the pro-rated minimum wage.
Three refinements:
The floor is pro-rated to contributory days, not applied in full to a part-month. An employee hired
on the 20th contributes on 11 days at the pro-rated floor, not on a full minimum wage.
Unpaid leave (change event SLN) reduces the contributory days. The contribution-return rate table
shows that in this case health moves to 8.5% or 0% and pension to 12% or 16% depending on the variant, and the payroll levies fall to 0%.
Part-time contributions. Law 2466 of 2025 art. 34 permits micro-enterprises and households to
pay social security on a part-time basis, computed in weeks and settled at each month end, and permits proportional contributions where monthly income is below the minimum wage. Access requires commercial registration, accounting books and an internal register of employment contracts. Explicitly outside standard corporate payroll scope, and monitored by the UGPP; the part-time contribution may not be used where the work is in fact full-time (art. 34 par. 2).
How are contributions calculated for Board of Directors who are also employees?
Colombia distinguishes sharply between a board mandate and an employment relationship, and the payroll treatment follows the substance of the relationship, not the title.
Situation
Employment relationship?
Social security
Withholding tax
Director who is also an employee — an executive director holding an employment contract
Yes
Full employee treatment on the whole remuneration, salary and director's fees alike, where the fees are consideration for the service. Employer 12% + 8.5% + occupational risk + levies; employee 4% + 4% + solidarity fund
Art. 383 progressive table
Non-executive director receiving only attendance fees (honorarios de junta directiva)
No — this is a corporate mandate, not employment
Not an employee contribution. The director contributes as an independent worker on a base of 40% of the monthly fee, with a 1-minimum-wage floor (Law 1955 of 2019 art. 244)
Art. 383 table applies — Tax Code art. 383 par. 2 extends the employment table to non-employment work income using "pagos mensualizados": total contract value less mandatory health and pension contributions, divided by the months of the contract
Statutory legal representative on an employment contract
Yes
Full employee treatment; generally management staff excluded from the maximum-hours rules
Art. 383 table
Two engine consequences.
Where a person is both an employee and a fee-earning director of the same or a related company, the
two income streams have different social-security bases — full salary for the employment, 40% of fees for the mandate — but the same withholding table. They accumulate against the shared 25-minimum-wage ceiling.
Directors' fees paid to a non-employee are outside payroll scope for social security and are handled
as an independent-contractor payment. Mercans scope covers the employment relationship only; the fee-only director is descoped and the client retains responsibility.
Are year-end adjustments required?
No year-end reconciliation of social security is required. Colombian contributions are definitively settled month by month. There is no annual return, no annual true-up, no reconciliation filing and no year-end adjustment mechanism for health, pension, occupational risk, the solidarity fund or the payroll levies. Twelve monthly contribution returns are the complete annual record.
What is required at or around year end:
Obligation
Timing

Severance-savings liquidation at 31 December and deposit to the employee's fund
By 14 February
Not a contribution — a statutory benefit deposit. Late deposit costs one day's salary per day of delay

Last business day of March
Tax
Corrections, not adjustments. Where a past month was under- or over-declared, the fix is a correction planilla (planilla type N), in which the employer reports paired records — an A group carrying the originally settled value and a C group carrying the corrected value. The sum of the contribution bases in the C records must be greater than or equal to that of the A records, and likewise for the day counts. The correction may only be filed through the same information operator that carried the original return, absent an authorisation from the financial superintendence. Under-declaration attracts late-payment interest and UGPP assessment exposure.
Legal basis: Law 100 of 1993 arts. 18, 20, 204 (as substituted by Law 797 of 2003 arts. 5 and 7, and Law 1122 of 2007 art. 10); Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30; Law 21 of 1982 arts. 9, 12, 17; Law 89 of 1988 art. 1; Law 1955 of 2019 art. 244; Law 2466 of 2025 arts. 34, 64; Tax Code arts. 114-1, 383 par. 2; Substantive Labour Code arts. 127, 128, 132, 143; Decree 1072 of 2015 arts. 2.2.1.2.1.4, 2.2.4.2.5.4, 2.2.4.2.5.6, 2.2.4.2.5.7, 2.2.4.3.3, 2.2.4.3.4, 2.2.4.3.5, 2.2.4.3.6; Decree 1772 of 1994 art. 13; Decree 768 of 2022; Decree-Law 2090 of 2003; Decree 780 of 2016 arts. 3.2.1.5 and 3.2.2.1 (substituted by Decree 1990 of 2016 arts. 1 and 2); Resolution MSPS 2388 of 2016 and Technical Annex 2; Decree 0223 of 5 March 2026 art. 2.2.6.3.3.4; Law 50 of 1990 art. 99 num. 3; Law 52 of 1975 art. 1. Not yet in force, recorded for the 2027 change-control only: Law 2381 of 2024 arts. 19, 20, 21, 22, 28, 75, 94; Constitutional Court Auto 841 of 18 June 2025; Constitutional Court Judgment C-264 of 25 August 2026.
5.3 Voluntary Contributions & Private Schemes
Can employees and employers make additional voluntary contributions?
Yes — through four distinct vehicles, each with its own tax character and its own cap. Distinguishing them correctly is essential, because two are non-taxable income and two are exempt income, and they consume different caps.
#
Vehicle
Who contributes
Tax character
Cap
1
Voluntary contributions to the private individual pension account (cotizaciones voluntarias al RAIS)
Employee, and employer on the employee's behalf
Non-taxable income (Tax Code art. 55)
25% of annual labour income and 2,500 tax units = COP 130,935,000
2
Voluntary pension fund contributions (aportes a fondos de pensiones voluntarias)
Employee and employer
Exempt income (Tax Code art. 126-1)
Shared 30%-of-labour-income / 3,800 tax units = COP 199,021,200 annual cap
3
Construction-savings and long-term-savings accounts (AFC / AVC)
Employee
Exempt income (Tax Code art. 126-4)
Shares the same 30% / 3,800-tax-unit cap with vehicle 2
4
Additional contributions to the public defined-benefit regime
Employee
Contribution, not a tax relief in itself
Within the 25-minimum-wage ceiling
Vehicle 1 — the individual-account voluntary contribution. Tax Code art. 55 treats these as ingresos no constitutivos de renta ni ganancia ocasional — non-taxable income deducted before the 40% and 1,340-tax-unit caps are applied, which makes them materially more valuable than exempt income. The cap is 25% of annual labour income, subject to an absolute 2,500 tax units. A withdrawal that does not meet the qualifying conditions is withheld at 35%.
Vehicles 2 and 3 — voluntary pension funds and long-term savings accounts. Tax Code arts. 126-1 and 126-4 make these rentas exentas — exempt income deducted inside the 40% and 1,340-tax-unit caps, and sharing a single combined annual limit of the lower of 30% of labour income or 3,800 tax units. Employer contributions to a voluntary pension fund are deductible for the employer up to 3,800 tax units per employee (art. 126-1 first paragraph).
Employer voluntary contributions to a voluntary pension fund on the employee's behalf are treated as the employee's own contribution for cap purposes and are exempt income in the employee's hands, within the same shared cap.
There is no voluntary top-up of health, occupational risk or the payroll levies. Those three are fixed statutory percentages with no voluntary element.
Which employee contributions are allowed as pre-tax deductions?
Colombia does not use the term "pre-tax deduction". It uses two distinct legal categories, and the distinction determines where in the depuration each item sits.
Category A — non-taxable income (deducted at step B, before the 40% cap):

Not deductible against withholding. The 1% / 240-tax-unit invoice-based deduction of Tax Code art. 336 num. 5 is expressly excluded from the withholding calculation — "shall not be taken into account for the calculation of withholding at source" — confirmed by DIAN Concepto 015007 (int. 973) of 13 September 2023. It is available only in the employee's own annual return.
Post-tax deductions with no tax effect: union dues, cooperative and savings-fund dues, payroll loans, insurance premiums other than qualifying health cover, and court-ordered garnishments.
Are there private insurance schemes with employer and employee contributions?
Yes. Four are common in the Colombian market. None is a statutory scheme, and none replaces a statutory contribution.

Payroll treatment. Employer-paid premiums are generally structured as non-salary under a written Labour Code art. 128 pact, which keeps them out of the contribution base, the payroll-levy base and the statutory-benefit base — subject always to the 40% clamp. Employee-paid premiums are a post-tax payroll deduction requiring the employee's written authorisation for each case (Labour Code art. 149), which may separately generate a withholding deduction under Category B above.
No occupational pension scheme exists in Colombia in the sense of a defined-benefit or defined contribution plan sponsored by the employer as an alternative to the state system. The mandatory pension system is universal, and the voluntary pension fund is an individual product to which an employer may contribute — not an employer-sponsored plan with its own trustees and rules.

Who counts as a dependant — Tax Code art. 387 par. 2, numerals 2 and 3 as amended by Law 2411 of 2024 art. 2: children up to 18; children aged 18 to 25 whose higher or technical education the taxpayer finances; children over 18 in a certified state of physical or psychological dependence; a spouse or permanent partner dependent through absence of income or annual income below 260 tax units, or through certified physical or psychological dependence; and parents and siblings on the same conditions.
A benefit-stacking point. Decree 2231 of 2023 art. 3 provides that a single dependant ordinarily gives rise to only one of the two dependant deductions — "except where the taxpayer has income from an employment or statutory and regulatory relationship, in which case both deductions may be applied for the same dependant". For an employee, therefore, the engine may take both the art. 387 dependant deduction (10% of gross, capped at 32 tax units per month) and the art. 336 additional 72 tax units per dependant, for the same dependant.
Legal basis: Tax Code (Decree-Law 624 of 1989) arts. 55, 56, 119, 126-1, 126-4, 206 num. 10, 336 nums. 3 and 5, 383 par. 3, 387, 387-1, 388; Law 2411 of 2024 art. 2; Decree 2231 of 2023 arts. 3 and 9; DIAN Concepto 015007 int. 973 of 13 September 2023; Substantive Labour Code arts. 128, 149; Decree 1072 of 2015 art. 2.2.1.2.1.4; Law 100 of 1993 art. 18.

Module 6: Income Tax Withholding System
Purpose: To define the entire income tax withholding system.
Resident / non-resident treatment matrix (control B1). Built before the prose because Colombia's treatment turns on tax residence in a way that is frequently mis-configured. All amounts in COP; tax unit (UVT) 2026 = 52,374; minimum wage 2026 = 1,750,905.
Income type
RESIDENT
NON-RESIDENT
Regular salary from a Colombian employer, work performed in Colombia
Progressive table, Tax Code art. 383 (0 / 19 / 28 / 33 / 35 / 37 / 39%), applied to the art. 388 depurated monthly base. Method: Procedure 1 or Procedure 2
Flat 20%, Tax Code art. 408, applied to the gross payment. No art. 388 depuration, no 25% exempt-income deduction, no dependants or other art. 387 deductions, no tax-free band. Confirmed by DIAN Concepto 011108 of 29 June 2026, which upholds Concepto 1789 (int. 14595) of 2025

Flat 20% under art. 408, on the gross bonus. No separate treatment and no separate table read

Exempt in full where the average monthly income of the last 6 months ≤ 350 tax units (18,330,900); tapered above (Tax Code art. 206 num. 4). Excluded from the monthly withholding base under both procedures
Flat 20% under art. 408 on the gross amount. The art. 206 num. 4 exemption is an exemption of the resident cédula-general regime and does not attach to a non-resident's flat withholding

Fully taxable employment income; enters the ordinary monthly base. Only the statutory-minimum indemnities of art. 206 nums. 1–3 are exempt, and only up to the legal minimum amount (art. 206 par. 1)
Flat 20% under art. 408

Taxable employment income at exercise or vesting, on the spread between market value and exercise price; enters the ordinary monthly base and the art. 383 table. Subsequent disposal is a separate capital transaction, outside payroll
Flat 20% under art. 408 where the underlying service was performed in Colombia. Where the service was performed abroad, foreign-source and not taxable
Labour-service / contractor pay (non-employment work income)
Art. 383 table applies, using "pagos mensualizados" — total contract value less mandatory health and pension contributions, divided by the months of the contract (art. 383 par. 2, added by Law 2277 of 2022 art. 8)
Flat 20% under art. 408 for services rendered in Colombia. Technical services, technical assistance and consultancy rendered from abroad are also subject to withholding under arts. 24 and 408
Benefits in kind (salary in kind, employer-paid housing, cars, insurance)
Valued contractually or by expert appraisal; fully taxable as employment income within the ordinary monthly base. No notional valuation scale exists

Not income up to 41 tax units per month (2,147,334) where salary ≤ 310 tax units (16,235,940); the excess is taxable (art. 387-1)
The art. 387-1 exclusion is drafted as a non-income rule for the worker and applies on its own terms; any excess is withheld at the flat 20%

Taxable only above 1,000 tax units per month (52,374,000) — art. 206 num. 5
Flat 20%
The residence switch is prospective, never retroactive. DIAN Concepto 011108 of 2026 rejected the argument that a foreign employee on a contract exceeding 183 days may be withheld under art. 383 from day one in anticipation of future residence. Withholding is determined at the moment of each payment, on the status then held. The engine must therefore withhold at 20% until residence is acquired, and switch to the art. 383 table from the payment following the date the 183-day test is met. There is no true-up of the earlier months through payroll; the employee reconciles in their own annual return.
Non-residents are IN SCOPE for Mercans Colombia payroll. A foreign national employed under a Colombian contract before acquiring residence is a real and common case (expatriate assignments, regional roles). The engine must carry a residence flag with an effective date and must support the mid-year switch.
6.1 System Overview and Methodology
Describe the country's income tax withholding system.
Colombia operates a monthly, employer-administered advance-collection system called retención en la fuente sobre rentas de trabajo — withholding at source on work income. Its defining characteristics:
Characteristic
Colombia
Nature
Advance instalment of the employee's own annual income tax, not a final tax
Frequency
Monthly — one aggregated return per employer per month
Cumulative?
No. Non-cumulative. Each month stands alone under Procedure 1; Procedure 2 uses a fixed percentage recomputed twice a year
Employer-borne income tax?
None. Colombia has no employer payroll tax on income. The employer's cost is the social-security and levy burden of Module 5 only
Basis
A depurated monthly figure, neither gross pay nor the social-security contribution base
Rate structure
Progressive, expressed in tax units — seven bands, 0% to 39%
Employee election
The employee may request a higher rate in writing; there is no election of a lower rate
Employer election
The employer elects Procedure 1 or Procedure 2 per employee; once elected for a semester, Procedure 2 binds for six months
Reconciliation
The employee's own annual income-tax return, if required to file. The employer performs no year-end true-up
Return
Form 350, monthly, aggregating all withholdings of every type made by the employer
Certificate
Form 220, annual income and withholding certificate to each employee
The employer is an agente de retención — a withholding agent. Its statutory obligations are to withhold the correct amount, to declare it on Form 350 by the deadline, to pay it, to issue the annual certificate, and to report the detail in the third-party information return.
What types of taxes are withheld by the employer?
For a standard employment relationship the employer withholds one tax only.
Withheld item
Applies to payroll?
Basis
Income tax withholding on work income (retención en la fuente sobre rentas de trabajo)
Yes — the only payroll withholding
Tax Code arts. 383, 385, 386, 388 (resident); arts. 408–415 (non-resident)
Value-added tax withholding
No — supplier payments, not payroll
Tax Code art. 437-2
Industry and commerce tax withholding
No — municipal, on business turnover, not on salary
Municipal statutes
Stamp duty
No
Tax Code art. 519
Financial-transactions tax
No — bank-level, on movements of funds
Tax Code art. 870
Solidarity contribution to higher education
Deducted, not a tax — a Labour Code art. 150 permitted deduction requiring no individual order, added by Law 1911 of 2018 art. 22
Labour Code art. 150
There is no local, municipal, departmental, church, solidarity or social surcharge on employment income in Colombia. The solidarity pension fund contribution (Module 5) is a social-security contribution, not a tax, and is itself non-taxable income to the employee.
At what level are tax rates published (central or state)?
Wholly central. Income tax and its withholding are national taxes, legislated by Congress in the Tax Code and administered by DIAN. There is:
no departmental or municipal income tax on employment income;
no sub-national rate, band, allowance or surcharge;
no local variation of the tax unit.
The two administrative acts that set the annual parameters are both national:
Parameter
Instrument
2026
Tax unit (UVT)
Resolution DIAN issued each December under Tax Code art. 868
Resolution DIAN 000238 of 15 December 2025 — COP 52,374
Consolidated tax, customs and exchange rules
The Single Tax Resolution
Resolution DIAN 000227 of 23 September 2025
The tax unit is uprated annually by the consumer price index for middle-income households over the year to 1 October. For 2026 that variation was certified at 5.17%, taking the unit from COP 49,799 to COP 52,374.
Legal basis: Tax Code (Decree-Law 624 of 1989) arts. 365, 368, 383, 385, 386, 388, 408–415, 437-2, 519, 868, 870; Resolution DIAN 000238 of 15 December 2025 art. 1; Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025; Substantive Labour Code art. 150 (as amended by Law 1911 of 2018 art. 22); DIAN Concepto 011108 of 29 June 2026; DIAN Oficio 28380 of 2019.
6.1.1 Taxable Income Definition
Provide the statutory definition of 'Taxable Income'.
The taxable base for withholding is the depurated payment of Tax Code article 388, added by Law 1819 of 2016 art. 18:
"For the purposes of withholding at source, the payer must subtract from the payments or credits to account the following factors: 1. Income that the law exhaustively provides as not constituting taxable income or occasional gain. 2. The deductions referred to in article 387 of the Tax Code and the income that the law exhaustively provides as exempt. In any event, the total sum of deductions and exempt income may not exceed forty percent (40%) of the result of subtracting, from the amount of the payment or credit to account, the attributable income that does not constitute taxable income or occasional gain. This limitation shall not apply in the case of the payment of pensions… pension substitution indemnities and refunds of pension savings."
An absolute annual cap is added by regulation — Decree 1625 of 2016 art. 1.2.4.1.6 par. 3, as modified by Decree 2231 of 2023 art. 9: deductions and exempt income may not exceed the particular limit of each benefit, nor 40%, nor the absolute value of 1,340 tax units per year (COP 70,181,160 for 2026).
The scope of art. 383 defines what enters the base in the first place: "the taxable payments made … arising from an employment relationship, or a statutory and regulatory relationship, and payments received in respect of retirement, disability, old-age, survivors' and occupational-risk pensions."
Explain how this base is derived from total gross earnings.
(A)  TOTAL TAXABLE PAYMENTS OF THE MONTH     basic salary + overtime + night and rest-day premiums + commissions     + habitual bonuses + salary in kind + transport allowance     + service bonus [Procedure 2 only; Procedure 1 reads it separately]     EXCLUDING: severance savings and the interest on them (both procedures)(B)  LESS NON-TAXABLE INCOME  (ingresos no constitutivos de renta ni ganancia ocasional)     - employee mandatory PENSION contribution 4%                    art. 55     - employee SOLIDARITY PENSION FUND contribution 1% + 0.2-1.0%   art. 55     - employee mandatory HEALTH contribution 4%                     art. 56     - voluntary contributions to the individual pension account,       <= 25% of annual labour income, cap 2,500 tax units           art. 55     - qualifying third-party meal payments                          art. 387-1(C)  = (A) - (B)          <-- the reference figure for the 40% cap(D)  LESS DEDUCTIONS  (art. 387)     - housing-loan interest and monetary correction   monthly cap 100 tax units = 5,237,400     - prepaid health / health insurance               monthly cap  16 tax units =   837,984     - dependants: 10% of the month's gross labour income,                                                       monthly cap  32 tax units = 1,675,968(E)  LESS EXEMPT INCOME  (rentas exentas)     - voluntary pension funds + long-term savings accounts:       <= 30% of labour income, annual cap 3,800 tax units = 199,021,200   arts. 126-1, 126-4     - THEN, AND ONLY THEN, the 25% exemption of art. 206 num. 10,       computed on [(C) - D - other exempt income], annual cap 790 tax units = 41,375,460(F)  APPLY THE COMBINED CAP     (D + E) <= 40% of (C)     AND     (D + E) <= 1,340 tax units annually = 70,181,160     OUTSIDE the cap: 72 tax units per dependant per year, max 4 dependants                      = 3,770,928 each   (art. 336 num. 3, second paragraph)(G)  TAXABLE BASE = (C) - (D + E as capped) - (72-unit dependant deduction)(H)  CONVERT TO TAX UNITS:  base_UVT = (G) / 52,374(I)  APPLY THE ART. 383 TABLE  ->  withholding in tax units  ->  x 52,374  ->  COP
The single most important ordering rule. Tax Code art. 206 num. 10 requires the 25% exemption to be computed "once there are subtracted from the total value of the labour payments received by the employee the income that does not constitute taxable income, the deductions and the other exempt income items other than the one established in the present numeral". Computing 25% of gross rather than 25% of the post-deduction figure overstates the exemption and under-withholds. It is computed last.
Which tax-unit vintage applies to the annual caps. DIAN Concepto 015007 (int. 973) of 13 September 2023 confirms that the current year's tax unit is used — COP 52,374 for 2026 — "a clarification that is applicable for the purposes of procedure two … and the calculation of the fixed withholding percentage that must be carried out in the month of June".
Open item — monthly apportionment of annual caps. The 790-tax-unit and 1,340-tax-unit caps are stated annually, yet withholding is monthly. Market practice, which Mercans follows, is to apportion them at one twelfth per month — 65.8333 tax units (COP 3,447,955) and 111.6667 tax units (COP 5,848,430) respectively — and to test each month against the apportioned figure. No regulation prescribes the apportionment expressly. Carried in the QA report.

It overrides upward only, never downward.

6.2 Tax Rates and Logic

Note on the tax-unit denominated bands. Because the bands are in tax units, they reprice automatically each year. The 2026 boundaries in pesos are set out in the table in Module 6.1.1.
How are rates applied for different frequencies?
The withholding table is a monthly table, and the rates do not change with frequency. What changes is how a non-monthly period is mapped onto it.
Frequency
Procedure 1
Procedure 2
Monthly
Read the table directly on the month's depurated base
Apply the fixed percentage to the month's depurated base
Semi-monthly
Common Colombian practice pays the first half as an untaxed advance and computes the full month's withholding on the second payment. The statutorily rigorous alternative — grossing each half-month to 30 days and applying the resulting percentage to the actual half-month payments — is available under art. 385 and produces a smoother net
Apply the fixed percentage to each payment, or to the month's total at month end
Weekly / ten-day / fourteen-day
Art. 385, sub-30-day rule: divide the payments by the number of days, multiply by 30, read the table percentage, then apply that percentage to the actual payments of the period
Apply the fixed percentage to each payment
A period longer than a month (arrears covering several months)
See Module 9.2
See Module 9.2
The art. 385 sub-30-day rule is worth restating because it is a percentage rule, not an amount rule. The table is read to obtain a rate on the grossed-up notional monthly figure, and that rate is then applied to the real, smaller payment. Reading the table for an amount on the grossed-up figure would grossly over-withhold.
