Applies — Colombia's treaty network (Spain, Chile, Switzerland, Canada, Mexico, South Korea, Portugal, India, Czech Republic, United Kingdom, France, Italy, Japan, and the Andean Community Decision 578) can reduce or eliminate the 20% where the employment article assigns taxing rights to the residence state. Treaty relief is a client decision requiring a residence certificate; Mercans applies it only on written client instruction
The residence test — Tax Code art. 10. Presence exceeding 183 calendar days, continuous or not, in any 365-day period; where the period straddles two years, residence attaches to the year in which the 183rd day falls. Plus the diplomatic and nationality-plus-connection tests set out in Module 2.2.
Operational rule. Carry a residence flag with an effective date on the employee record; re-evaluate it every payroll period; switch the schedule prospectively from the payment following the date the test is met. Never apply the change retroactively through payroll.
Provide the tax calculation logic covering eligibility, joining month, termination month, mid-month joining, mid-month terminations, and mid-month salary revisions.
Eligibility. Every employee is within the withholding system from the first payment. There is no registration threshold, no minimum-earnings exclusion and no employee opt-out. An employee whose depurated base falls in the 0% band is still within the system — the withholding is simply nil, and the payment is still reported on Form 350, in the electronic payroll XML, in the annual certificate and in the information return.
Joining month and mid-month joining.
Procedure
Rule
Procedure 1
Apply the art. 385 sub-30-day rule: (payments ÷ days) × 30 → read the table percentage → apply that percentage to the actual payments of the period
Procedure 2
A new hire has no 12-month history, so no fixed percentage exists. Art. 386 directs that until the first semi-annual calculation the employer reads the table on the month's own taxable payments — i.e. Procedure 1 behaviour. At the next June or December calculation, the average uses the actual months of service as the divisor, not 13
Termination month and mid-month termination.
Compute the withholding on the salary and other ordinary payments for the days worked, using the same
sub-30-day rule under Procedure 1 or the fixed percentage under Procedure 2.
Exclude severance savings and the interest on them from the monthly base under both procedures; they
carry the art. 206 num. 4 exemption and taper, driven by the average monthly income of the last six months of employment.
Under Procedure 1, the accrued statutory minimum service bonus paid on termination retains its
separate table read.
A dismissal indemnity is fully taxable ordinary employment income and enters the monthly base.
There is no termination-year true-up and no final tax reconciliation by the employer. The annual
certificate (Form 220) records what was paid and what was withheld; the employee reconciles.
Mid-month salary revision.
Procedure
Rule
Procedure 1
The month's payments are simply summed, whatever the segments, and the table is read once on the combined depurated base. No splitting is required for tax — unlike for social security, where a salary-variation change event is reported
Procedure 2
The fixed percentage is unchanged until the next June or December recalculation. A large mid-year increase therefore produces under-withholding for the remainder of the semester. The employee's remedy is a written request for a higher rate under art. 383 par. 3; the employer may not unilaterally increase the fixed percentage mid-semester
A change of procedure. The procedure is elected by the employer, per employee. Procedure 2, once elected, applies for the whole six-month period following the June or December calculation. Market practice is to change procedure only at a semester boundary.
Do tax rates vary by gender or age?
No. There is no gender-differentiated or age-differentiated rate, band, allowance, deduction or exemption anywhere in the Colombian income-tax withholding system. The art. 383 table, the art. 387 deductions, the art. 206 exemptions and the art. 336 caps are identical for every employee.
Two adjacent points that are sometimes mistaken for age variation:
Pension income is taxable only on the monthly amount exceeding 1,000 tax units (COP 52,374,000)
— Tax Code art. 206 num. 5. That is a rule about the type of income, not about the recipient's age, and it applies only where the taxpayer meets the Law 100 pension-entitlement conditions.
Dependants aged 18 to 25 in higher or technical education generate a deduction for the taxpayer
(Tax Code art. 387 par. 2 num. 2). That is a characteristic of the dependant, not of the employee.
What is the minimum tax rate applicable?
Zero per cent — for residents. The first band of the art. 383 table is 0% up to 95 tax units per month, which for 2026 is COP 4,975,530 of depurated base. There is no minimum withholding, no floor amount and no minimum-tax rule in the withholding system.
Two important qualifications:
95 tax units of depurated base is a considerably higher gross salary. Working the arithmetic
backwards for an employee with only the mandatory contributions and the 25% exemption: a depurated base of 4,975,530 corresponds to a pre-exemption figure of 4,975,530 ÷ 0.75 = 6,634,040, which grossed up for the 9% employee contributions (4% + 4% + 1%) is approximately COP 7,290,000 of monthly gross salary. In practice, therefore, an employee earning up to roughly 7.3 million pesos a month — about 4.2 minimum wages — suffers no withholding at all.
For non-residents the minimum is 20%, from the first peso. There is no exempt band, no allowance and
no minimum threshold under art. 408.
Is there a maximum? The top marginal rate is 39% above 2,300 tax units per month (COP 120,460,200). Since the base is depurated and progressive, the effective rate never reaches 39%.
Employee-elected uplift. Tax Code art. 383 par. 3 permits the employee to request a higher withholding rate in writing, effective from the month after the request. This is used by employees who expect a balance due on their annual return. The engine must support an employee-elected rate that overrides upward only.
Legal basis: Tax Code (Decree-Law 624 of 1989) arts. 10, 206 nums. 4 and 5, 383 (and pars. 1, 2, 3), 385, 386, 387, 388, 408–415, 592; Law 2010 of 2019 art. 42; Law 2277 of 2022 art. 8; DIAN Concepto 011108 of 29 June 2026; DIAN Oficio 28380 of 2019; Andean Community Decision 578 and Colombia's bilateral double-tax treaties.
6.3 Adjustments and Special Cases
For annualized tax systems, which salary components must be projected?
Colombia is not an annualised or cumulative withholding system, so there is no projection of future income. Procedure 1 looks only at the current month. Procedure 2 looks only backwards, at the preceding 12 months.
This is a genuine structural difference from cumulative jurisdictions and must be configured deliberately: there is no annual estimate, no notional annual salary, no projected bonus and no year-end catch-up.
What Procedure 2 requires — a backward-looking accumulator, not a projection:
In JUNE and in DECEMBER of each year:  numerator = the sum of ALL taxable payments made to the employee over the              preceding 12 months, EXCLUDING severance savings and the              interest on severance savings  divisor   = 13          (or the actual number of months of service, if < 12)  average   = numerator / divisor  fixed %   = the art. 383 table result for `average`, expressed as a              percentage of `average`   (art. 383 par. 1)  The fixed % is then applied to each of the FOLLOWING SIX MONTHS' depurated bases.
The divisor is 13, not 12. Tax Code art. 386 says "dividir por 13". The 13 divisor prorates the 13th-month effect of the statutory service bonus — which, unlike under Procedure 1, is not given a separate table read under Procedure 2 and instead sits inside the ordinary base. Using 12 over-withholds by roughly 8%.
Components that must therefore be accumulated for 12 rolling months:
Component
In the accumulator?
Basic salary
Yes
Overtime, night and rest-day premiums
Yes
Commissions and habitual bonuses
Yes
Statutory service bonus
Yes
Salary in kind
Yes
Transport allowance
Yes
Vacation pay and cash-compensated vacation
Yes
Severance savings
No — expressly excluded by art. 386
Interest on severance savings
No — expressly excluded
Non-taxable income and exempt income
Deducted in the depuration, per art. 387 par. 1, which requires the art. 387 deductions to be used both in computing the fixed percentage and in computing the monthly base it is applied to
Are year-end tax adjustments required?
No — not by the employer. Colombia imposes no employer year-end tax reconciliation. The employer does not compute an annual liability, does not compare it with withholdings, and does not settle a difference through payroll. There is no equivalent of a P60 reconciliation or a year-end true-up run.
What the employer must do at year end is certify and report, not reconcile:
Obligation
Deadline
Content
Form 220 — annual income and withholding certificate to each employee
Last business day of March
Tax Code arts. 378–379: the employer's and employee's identification, the total payments by concept, the total withheld, and the employee's own declarations where the employee is a non-filer (art. 380). Form prescribed by Resolution DIAN 000022 of 20 February 2024
Information return, Format 2276 version 4 — income and withholdings on work income
May–June, by tax number
Resolution DIAN 000227 of 2025 arts. 1.3.7.1.1 and 1.3.9.1
December recalculation of the fixed percentage (Procedure 2 only)
December
Sets the rate for January–June of the following year
Reparameterisation for the new tax unit
1 January
Every band boundary and cap reprices
The employee reconciles, not the employer. An employee whose income exceeds the Tax Code art. 592 filing thresholds files an annual income-tax return, in which the withholdings are credited against the computed liability and any excess is refunded or any shortfall paid.
Are there minimum or higher tax requirements if employees fail to submit a tax ID or social security number?
No. Colombia has no penalty withholding rate for a missing tax identifier — no equivalent of an emergency code, a flat penalty rate or a default maximum rate.
The reasons are structural:
Employees are generally not required to be registered in the tax registry. The employer's withholding
obligation arises from the payment, not from the employee's registration status. The employee's identity-document number is the identifier used throughout.
The identity document is a precondition of employment itself, not of payroll. An employment contract
requires an identified person; social-security affiliation requires an identity document; the electronic payroll XML will not validate without a document type and number from the closed code list.
What actually happens where identifiers are missing or wrong:
Missing item
Consequence
Identity document type or number
The electronic payroll XML fails validation (rule ZB01 and the global rules). The document cannot be transmitted, so the employer loses the tax deductibility of that payroll cost. Contribution-return filing also fails
Employer tax number or verification digit
Validation rules NIE038 / NIE041 reject the document
Institution codes (health insurer, pension administrator, occupational-risk insurer, fund)
The contribution return fails or misdirects contributions
Employee declarations for deductions (dependants, housing interest, prepaid health)
No penalty — the employee simply loses the deduction and is over-withheld. The remedy is the employee's own annual return
Dependant's identity document
The deduction is not sustainable on audit, and the information return is rejected
The one direction in which the rate can move upward is voluntary: the employee may request a higher rate in writing under art. 383 par. 3.
What is the income tax and social security refund procedure?
Colombia has two distinct refund routes, one for tax and one for contributions, and neither is a payroll operation in the ordinary case.
(a) Over-withheld income tax.
Route
Who initiates
Mechanism
Within the same fiscal year, before the Form 350 is filed
Employer
The employer corrects the withholding in a subsequent period of the same year and adjusts the aggregated Form 350 figure. This is the normal, and preferred, route
After the Form 350 has been filed
Employer
File a correction return for the affected period. Where the correction reduces the declared value, Tax Code art. 589 applies and the correction must be filed within one year of the original filing deadline
Reimbursement of amounts withheld in excess or wrongly withheld (retenciones practicadas en exceso o indebidamente)
Employee requests, employer executes
Decree 1625 of 2016 art. 1.2.4.16: on the employee's written request with evidence, the employer reimburses the excess and deducts the reimbursed amount from the withholdings of the following period, carrying any surplus forward until absorbed. The employer must keep the employee's written request
After the year has closed and the employee has filed
Employee
The excess is credited on the employee's own annual return and refunded by the tax authority under the general refund procedure (Tax Code arts. 850 et seq.)
(b) Over-paid social-security contributions.
Route
Mechanism
Correction planilla (type N)
The employer files paired records — an A group with the originally settled values and a C group with the corrected values. Note the structural constraint: the sum of the contribution bases and of the day counts in the C records must be greater than or equal to those in the A records, so the correction planilla is designed for under-declaration, not over-declaration
Refund claim to the administering entity
For a genuine overpayment, the claim is made directly to the health insurer, pension administrator, occupational-risk insurer, family compensation fund, apprenticeship service or family welfare institute that received the money. Each has its own procedure and evidentiary requirements
Reimbursement to the employee
Where the employee's share was over-deducted, the employer reimburses the employee through payroll and pursues the recovery from the receiving entity itself. The employer carries the cash-flow and the process risk
UGPP
Where the overpayment arises from an audit adjustment, the UGPP process governs
Timing. Contribution refunds are materially slower than tax corrections — commonly several months — because each receiving entity processes independently. Mercans practice is therefore to reimburse the employee promptly through payroll on the employer's authority and to pursue recovery separately.

71.4002
(I) Band
≤ 95 tax units → 0%

Withholding

COP 0.00
Note the two ordering effects this example demonstrates: the 25% exemption is computed on the post-deduction figure (4,580,000, not 7,280,000), and the 72-unit-per-dependant deduction is applied after and outside the 40% cap. Both children generate both the art. 387 deduction and the art. 336 deduction, per Decree 2231 of 2023 art. 3.
Example 3 — non-resident employee, August 2026.
Facts: Spanish national, hired on a Colombian indefinite-term contract on 1 July 2026, working in Bogotá, salary COP 15,000,000 per month. As at the August payroll the employee has been present approximately 45 days and is not yet a Colombian tax resident.

The switch. The employee reaches 183 days of presence on or about 30 December 2026. From the first payment after that date the engine switches to the art. 383 progressive table. Had the same employee been a resident in August, the withholding would have been COP 1,190,532 rather than COP 3,000,000 — depurated base COP 10,237,500 = 195.4691 tax units, in the 28% band. No retroactive adjustment is made through payroll; the employee reconciles in their own annual return. Where the Colombia–Spain double-tax treaty assigns the taxing right differently, relief is applied only on written client instruction supported by a residence certificate.

Module 7: Deductions, Exemptions & Benefits
Purpose: To define rules for all items that adjust income or pay.
7.1 Statutory Tax Reliefs, Deductions, and Exemptions
Deliverable: For EACH statutory tax relief, deduction, exemption, or other tax advantage, provide Name and Definition, Eligibility Rules, Statutory Limits & Amounts, Calculation Method, Application Point, Employee Declaration Requirements
Colombia's reliefs fall into three legally distinct classes, and the class determines where each item sits in the depuration and which caps it consumes. Getting the class wrong is the most common source of withholding error.

The employee's compulsory 4% pension contribution on the social-security contribution base
Eligibility
Every employee who contributes to pension. Not available to an employed old-age pensioner, who makes no pension contribution

4% × contribution base, rounded up to the nearest 100
Application point
Step (B), non-taxable income

None — computed by the engine

Element
Detail
Definition
Voluntary contributions made by the employee, or by the employer on the employee's behalf, to the employee's individual account in the private pension regime
Eligibility
Employees affiliated to the private individual-account regime
Limit
The lower of 25% of annual labour income and 2,500 tax units = COP 130,935,000 per year

Deducted at the amount contributed, within the cap
Application point
Step (B) — more valuable than exempt income, because it is deducted before the 40% cap reference figure is struck
Declaration
Amount and period, evidenced by a pension administrator certificate
Clawback
A withdrawal that does not meet the qualifying conditions is withheld at 35%
Source
Tax Code art. 55
A5 — Third-party meal payments
Element
Detail
Definition
Payments made by the employer to a third party for the food of the employee or the employee's family, including the purchase of meal vouchers or tickets. "Familia" means the spouse or permanent partner, the children and the parents of the employee
Eligibility
Two independent conditions, both must hold: (i) the employee's salary does not exceed 310 tax units = COP 16,235,940; and (ii) the payment is made to a third party — cash paid to the employee is salary and gets none of this treatment
Limit
Where the payments in the month exceed 41 tax units = COP 2,147,334, the excess is taxable employment income subject to withholding
Calculation
Up to 41 tax units: not income to the employee, income to the third party supplying the food. Above 41 tax units: the excess enters the taxable base
Application point
Step (B) as a non-income exclusion
Declaration
Employer records; the information return reports the within-limit amount (field 32) and the excess (field 8) separately
Excluded
Representation expenses are expressly outside this rule and are deductible for the employer
Source
Tax Code art. 387-1
B1 — Housing-loan interest and monetary correction
Element
Detail
Definition
Interest and monetary correction paid on a loan for the acquisition of the employee's dwelling, or on a housing leasing contract
Eligibility
Employees paying qualifying housing-loan interest
Limit
Monthly cap 100 tax units = COP 5,237,400 (the monthly figure is derived from the annual 1,200-unit limit of Tax Code art. 119; the monthly apportionment is market practice — see the QA report)
Calculation
The lower of the amount paid and the cap
Application point
Step (D), inside the 40% and 1,340-unit caps

Amount paid, evidenced by a lender certificate

B5 — The 25% exempt-income deduction
Element
Detail
Definition
The general labour-income exemption of twenty-five per cent of total labour payments — "Twenty-five percent (25%) of the total value of the labour payments"
Eligibility
Every resident employee. Not available to a non-resident withheld under art. 408
Limit
790 tax units per year = COP 41,375,460; apportioned monthly at 65.8333 units = COP 3,447,955
Calculation
CRITICAL ORDERING — it is computed after every other item has been deducted: "The calculation of this exempt income shall be made once there are subtracted from the total value of the labour payments received by the employee the income that does not constitute taxable income, the deductions and the other exempt income items other than the one established in the present numeral." It is computed LAST, on the figure net of A, B1–B4
Application point
Step (E), last item, then subject to the caps
Declaration
None — automatic
Excluded
Does not apply to severance savings, to income already excluded by other rules, or to the taxable part of a pension (art. 206 par. 2)
Source
Tax Code art. 206 num. 10, as amended by Law 2277 of 2022 art. 2

Severance savings and their interest are excluded from the monthly withholding base entirely, under both procedures (Tax Code arts. 385 and 386). The exemption and taper are applied when the payment is made

C1 — The additional dependants deduction
Element
Detail
Definition
A deduction of 72 tax units per dependant per year, maximum 4 dependants
Eligibility
Dependants as defined in art. 387 par. 2
Limit
72 tax units = COP 3,770,928 per dependant per year; maximum 4 dependants = COP 15,083,712 per year; monthly 6 tax units = COP 314,244 per dependant
Calculation
Deducted after and outside the 40% and 1,340-unit caps — "in addition to the limit established in the preceding paragraph"
Application point
Step (G), outside the caps
Declaration
Number of dependants and each dependant's identity document, relationship and basis of dependency. The information return (field 34) requires the document, not merely a count
Stacking
May be taken together with B3 for the same dependant. Decree 2231 of 2023 art. 3: "the same dependant shall give rise to only one of these two deductions, except where the taxpayer has income from an employment or statutory and regulatory relationship, in which case both deductions may be applied for the same dependant"
Source
Tax Code art. 336 num. 3, second paragraph; Decree 2231 of 2023 art. 3
Not available against withholding. The 1% / 240-tax-unit invoice-based deduction of Tax Code art. 336 num. 5 is expressly excluded — "shall not be taken into account for the calculation of withholding at source" — confirmed by DIAN Concepto 015007 (int. 973) of 13 September 2023: "this deduction is confined to the determination of the net income of the general basket and does not apply for the purposes of withholding at source." It is available only in the employee's own annual return.
Outside the caps by statute, but outside Mercans scope. Tax Code art. 206 par. 4 and Decree 1625 of 2016 art. 1.2.4.1.6 par. 3 place numerals 6 to 9 of art. 206, the prima especial and the prima de costo de vida outside the 40% and 1,340-unit limits. These attach to military, judicial, diplomatic and public university staff — out of Mercans private-sector scope — but the engine must carry the flag so that it does not silently clamp them if such an employee is loaded.
Are salary sacrifice arrangements allowed?
There is no salary-sacrifice regime in Colombian law, and the nearest Colombian equivalents work in a materially different way.
Arrangement
Available?
Effect
Classic salary sacrifice — the employee gives up contractual salary in exchange for a non-cash benefit, reducing both the tax and the contribution base
No. There is no statutory mechanism, no tax-authority-approved structure and no doctrine supporting it. Labour Code art. 13 makes the minimum labour rights irrenounceable, and art. 143 presumes any differential remuneration unjustified
—
Non-salary pact (Labour Code art. 128) — the parties agree in writing that a benefit or allowance is not salary
Yes, and this is the Colombian workhorse. But it is not a sacrifice: the employee gives up nothing, and the payment must genuinely be a benefit or allowance, not consideration for the service
Removes the payment from the statutory-benefit base, the overtime base and the contribution base — but only up to the 40% clamp of Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30. No tax effect whatsoever: the payment remains fully taxable employment income
Employer contributions to voluntary pension funds
Yes
Exempt income to the employee within the shared 30% / 3,800-unit cap; deductible for the employer up to 3,800 units per employee
Employer contributions to the individual pension account
Yes
Non-taxable income to the employee within the 25% / 2,500-unit cap
Third-party meal payments and vouchers
Yes
Not income to the employee within the art. 387-1 thresholds
Salary in kind (Labour Code art. 129)
Yes, but it is the opposite of a sacrifice — it is salary, fully contributory and fully taxable, capped at 50% of total salary and 30% at the minimum wage

All-inclusive salary (Labour Code art. 132 num. 2)
Yes
Reduces the contribution and payroll-levy bases to 70% — the closest Colombia comes to a structural contribution reduction, available only above 13 minimum wages
The key configuration point. Colombian benefit structuring is a social-security optimisation, never a tax optimisation. A non-salary pact reduces contributions (within 40%) and statutory benefits; it does not reduce taxable income by one peso. Any engine that treats a non-salary flag as a tax exclusion will systematically under-withhold.

The attachability limits — Labour Code arts. 154–156:
Rule
Effect
Art. 154
Salary is not attachable as a general rule
Art. 155
The excess over the minimum wage is attachable up to one fifth (20%) of that excess
Art. 156
Exception: for maintenance obligations and debts owed to cooperatives, up to 50% of the whole salary, including the minimum-wage portion, is attachable
The overriding limitation — Labour Code art. 149 num. 2. No deduction or set-off may reduce pay below the minimum wage or into the non-attachable portion without a court order, even where the employee has authorised it in writing. This governs every voluntary deduction in the table, and is the reason priorities 5 to 8 carry "No" in the last column.
Payroll lending specifically. Law 1527 of 2012 governs libranza deductions. The employer must deduct and remit the instalment to the lender once the authorisation is in place; the aggregate of libranza deductions must leave the employee with at least 50% of net salary after all statutory deductions. Labour Code art. 149 num. 3 makes the employer liable in damages for failing to make an authorised, lawful deduction.

Genuine reimbursement of employer expenditure
General principle
Not exempt, contrary to a common misconception: the transport allowance, the connectivity allowance, the statutory service bonus, vacation pay, overtime and premiums, dismissal indemnities beyond the statutory-minimum indemnities of art. 206 nums. 1–3, and any bonus however labelled. A non-salary pact under Labour Code art. 128 confers no tax exemption of any kind.
Legal basis: Tax Code (Decree-Law 624 of 1989) arts. 55, 56, 119, 126-1, 126-4, 206 (nums. 1–5, 10 and pars. 1, 2, 4), 336 (nums. 3 and 5), 383, 385, 386, 387, 387-1, 388; Law 2277 of 2022 art. 2; Law 2411 of 2024 art. 2; Decree 1625 of 2016 art. 1.2.4.1.6 par. 3 (as modified by Decree 2231 of 2023 art. 9); Decree 2231 of 2023 art. 3; DIAN Concepto 015007 int. 973 of 13 September 2023; Substantive Labour Code arts. 13, 128, 129, 132, 143, 149, 150, 151, 154, 155, 156, 230 (art. 150 as amended by Law 1911 of 2018 art. 22); Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30; Law 1527 of 2012.
7.2 Benefits in Kind (BIK)
Are non-cash benefits taxed via the standard payroll system, or a separate employer-paid regime?
Through the standard payroll system. Colombia has no fringe-benefits tax, no separate employer-paid benefits regime and no parallel filing for non-cash benefits.
A benefit in kind is taxed by being classified. If it is salary in kind under Labour Code art. 129, it is ordinary taxable employment income and ordinary contributory salary, and it flows through the normal gross-to-net calculation. If it falls within Labour Code art. 128 or a specific tax exclusion, it is neither.
Question
Colombia
Separate fringe-benefits tax?
No
Separate employer-paid regime?
No
Separate return?
No — reported in the ordinary electronic payroll XML and information return
Notional or administrative valuation scales (company car tables, accommodation scales)?
None exist
Employer-borne or employee-borne?
Employee-borne — the employee is taxed on the value, and the employer withholds
What is the definition of fringe benefits and how is the taxable amount evaluated?
Labour Code art. 129 (Law 50 of 1990 art. 16), verbatim:
"1. Salary in kind consists of any part of the ordinary and permanent remuneration that the employee receives as direct consideration for the service, such as food, accommodation or clothing that the employer supplies to the employee or to their family, save for the stipulation provided in article [128] of this law. 2. Salary in kind must be expressly valued in every employment contract. In the absence of a stipulation or of agreement on its real value it shall be assessed by expert appraisal, without it being able to constitute more than fifty percent (50%) of the total salary. 3. However, where an employee earns the legal minimum wage, the value attributed to salary in kind may not exceed thirty percent (30%)."
Valuation method. Three rules, in order:
Contractual valuation. The value must be expressly stated in the employment contract. This is
the primary rule and the one the engine relies on.
Expert appraisal (estimación pericial) where the parties have not agreed a value or disagree on
the real value.
Statutory ceilings. Salary in kind may not exceed 50% of total salary, or 30% where the
employee earns the legal minimum wage.
There is no published valuation schedule for benefits in kind in Colombia. No company-car table, no accommodation scale, no notional-value list. This was searched for and none exists — and the absence is principled rather than accidental: art. 129 num. 2 routes an unvalued benefit to expert appraisal, not to a published scale. The engine therefore requires a contractual value on every in-kind wage type.
If a separate FBT regime exists, describe it in detail.
Not Applicable — no fringe-benefits tax regime exists in Colombia. Non-cash benefits are taxed to the employee through ordinary payroll withholding, or are excluded from income by a specific statutory rule. There is no employer-level benefits tax, no separate rate, no separate base and no separate return.
What is the calculation logic for fringe benefit tax?
Not Applicable — there is no fringe benefit tax. The calculation logic for a benefit in kind is therefore the ordinary classification decision tree:
For each benefit provided:Q1. Is it ordinary and permanent consideration for the service?    YES -> SALARY IN KIND (Labour Code art. 129)           * value it in the contract, or by expert appraisal           * cap: <= 50% of total salary;  <= 30% at the legal minimum wage           * enters the CONTRIBUTION base, the PAYROLL-LEVY base,             the STATUTORY-BENEFIT base           * FULLY TAXABLE within the ordinary monthly base    NO  -> Q2Q2. Is it a habitual or occasional benefit that the parties have EXPRESSLY    agreed IN WRITING is not salary?  (art. 128 - food, housing, clothing,    extra-statutory bonuses)    YES -> NOT SALARY for labour-law purposes           * out of the statutory-benefit base and the overtime base permanently           * out of the contribution base ONLY UP TO the 40% clamp;             the excess goes back in (Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30)           * STILL FULLY TAXABLE - a non-salary pact has NO tax effect    NO  -> Q3Q3. Is it given not to enrich the employee but to let them do the job -    representation expenses, means of transport, work tools, protective    equipment, statutory work clothing (art. 230)?    YES -> NOT SALARY and NOT TAXABLE. Outside every base.    NO  -> Q4Q4. Is it a MEAL benefit provided through a THIRD PARTY (restaurant,    voucher, meal card)?    YES -> Tax Code art. 387-1 regime:           * salary <= 310 tax units (16,235,940)  AND           * monthly amount <= 41 tax units (2,147,334)           -> not income to the employee; income to the third party           * excess over 41 tax units -> taxable employment income           * CASH paid to the employee for food is SALARY - none of this applies    NO  -> Q5Q5. Is it employer-paid PREPAID HEALTH or HEALTH INSURANCE?    YES -> not a statutory exclusion. It is a taxable benefit, but the           EMPLOYEE may deduct up to 16 tax units per month (837,984)           against the withholding base (art. 387 lits. a and b)    NO  -> Q6Q6. Is it an employer contribution to a VOLUNTARY PENSION FUND?    YES -> exempt income to the employee within the shared 30% /           3,800-tax-unit annual cap; deductible for the employer up to           3,800 tax units per employee (art. 126-1)    NO  -> default: taxable employment income, and salary unless a valid           written non-salary pact applies
Are benefits-in-kind also subject to withholding tax?
Yes — every taxable benefit in kind is subject to withholding, at the same progressive rates and through the same monthly calculation as cash pay. There is no separate rate and no separate mechanism.
Benefit
Withheld?
Salary in kind (food, housing, clothing as ordinary and permanent consideration)
Yes, on the contractual or appraised value
Company car for personal use
Yes, on the value attributable to the private benefit — valued contractually, since no scale exists
Employer-paid housing
Yes, on the contractual value
Employer-paid prepaid health / health insurance
Yes, on the premium — but the employee may deduct up to 16 tax units per month
Employer-paid group life or personal accident insurance
Yes, where the cover is personal to the employee
Non-salary-pacted benefits under art. 128
Yes — the pact has no tax effect
Third-party meal payments within the art. 387-1 thresholds
No
Third-party meal payments above 41 tax units
Yes, on the excess
Representation expenses, work tools, protective equipment, statutory work clothing
No
Employer contributions to voluntary pension funds
No, within the cap
The practical mechanic. A benefit in kind produces a positive earnings line with the benefit's value, which enters the taxable base, and — where the benefit is not paid in cash — an offsetting deduction line, so that net cash pay is unchanged while the withholding and, where applicable, the contributions are correctly computed. Where withholding on benefits would exceed available cash pay, see the minimum-wage protection rules in Module 4.1.

Reporting. The electronic payroll XML separates salary from non-salary at element level, with paired element names carrying an explicit S (salary) or NS (non-salary) suffix — salary and non-salary bonus, salary and non-salary allowance, salary and non-salary concept, salary and non-salary payment, salary and non-salary meal payment, salary and non-salary travel/accommodation allowance. The engine must therefore carry a hard salary/non-salary flag on every wage type, set at configuration, and must not attempt to derive it at reporting time.
Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 128, 129 (as amended by Law 50 of 1990 arts. 15 and 16), 230; Tax Code arts. 126-1, 206, 387 lits. a) and b), 387-1, 388; Law 1393 of 2010 art. 30; Resolution DIAN 000013 of 2021 and its Technical Annex.
7.3 Garnishments and Court Orders
What are the rules for processing statutory deductions like child support, court-ordered fines, or tax levies?
The controlling principle is Labour Code art. 149 num. 1: the employer "may not deduct, withhold or set off any sum from salary without an authorisation signed by the employee for each case, or without a court order." A garnishment therefore requires either a court order or, in the narrow case below, an administrative order with statutory force.
Type
Instrument
Processing
Maintenance / child support (alimentos)
Judicial order from a family judge, or a conciliation agreement approved by a family commissioner or the family welfare institute
Served on the employer, who becomes a retaining agent. Deduct from each payroll and remit to the beneficiary or to the court's deposit account. Up to 50% of the whole salary including the minimum-wage portion (art. 156)
Judicial attachment for a civil or commercial debt (embargo)
Court order in enforcement proceedings
Deduct up to 20% of the excess over the minimum wage (art. 155). Remit to the court's deposit account
Attachment for a cooperative debt
Court order
Up to 50% of the whole salary (art. 156)
Tax levy by the tax authority
Administrative enforcement (proceso de cobro coactivo) — the tax authority holds jurisdictional powers under Tax Code arts. 823 et seq. and may issue an attachment order without going to court
Treated as a court order for art. 149 purposes. The attachability limits of arts. 154–156 apply
Social-security enforcement by the contributions authority
Administrative enforcement by the UGPP
Same treatment
Court-ordered fines
Court order
Same treatment; art. 155 limits
Disciplinary sanctions
Not a garnishment — an employer deduction permitted by art. 150 only where imposed under a duly approved set of work rules
Subject to the minimum-wage floor
Employer duties on receipt of an order.
Acknowledge the order within the time it specifies and confirm the employment relationship and the
salary.
Apply the deduction from the next payroll falling due after service.
Remit to the account the order specifies, within the period it specifies.
Report any termination, unpaid leave or salary change that affects the order.
Keep the order and the remittance evidence for the retention period (Module 2.3).
Do not apply an order beyond its stated limit or duration, and do not apply an order served on
the employee rather than the employer.
Liability. Labour Code art. 149 num. 3 makes the employer "responsable de los perjuicios" for failing to make authorised, lawful deductions in good time. An employer that ignores a garnishment order can be held liable to the creditor for the amounts that should have been withheld.
Is there a statutory priority order if an employee has multiple garnishments?
Colombia has no single statutory garnishment-priority article, but a clear order emerges from the combination of the attachability rules, the general privilege of maintenance claims and the ordinary rules of civil enforcement. Mercans applies:
Priority
Claim
Limit
1
Mandatory social security and withholding tax
Statutory; applied before any garnishment
2
Maintenance obligations (alimentos)
Up to 50% of the whole salary (art. 156). Maintenance ranks first among garnishments — the obligation is of constitutional rank (Political Constitution art. 44, the prevailing rights of the child) and art. 156 gives it the widest reach
3
Debts owed to cooperatives
Also within the art. 156 50% reach, but ranking behind maintenance where both compete
4
Tax and social-security enforcement
Within the art. 155 limit — 20% of the excess over the minimum wage
5
Ordinary civil and commercial attachments, in date order of service on the employer
Within the art. 155 limit, shared among competing orders in date order
6
Payroll lending (libranza) and employee-authorised voluntary deductions
Never above the protected floor; the aggregate must leave at least 50% of net salary (Law 1527 of 2012)
Where competing orders exceed the ceiling. The employer applies orders in the priority above until the applicable ceiling is reached, then notifies the court or authority whose order cannot be satisfied, stating the competing orders and the amounts applied. The employer must not apportion pro rata on its own initiative, and must not exceed the ceiling to satisfy all orders.
Interaction between the two ceilings. Art. 156 (50% of the whole salary) and art. 155 (20% of the excess over the minimum wage) are different ceilings for different claim types, not cumulative allowances. A maintenance order may reach into the minimum-wage portion; a commercial attachment may not. Where both are in play, the maintenance order is applied first within its own 50% ceiling, and the commercial attachment is then applied only to the extent that the art. 155 headroom on the excess over the minimum wage remains unused.
Are there protected earnings limits?
Yes — Colombia protects salary robustly, with one broad exception.
Rule
Protection
Labour Code art. 154
"Salary is exempt from attachment" — salary is not attachable as the general rule
Labour Code art. 155
The excess over the minimum wage is attachable only up to one fifth (20%) of that excess. So an employee on COP 3,000,000: the excess over 1,750,905 is 1,249,095, and the maximum attachable is 20% of that = COP 249,819
Labour Code art. 156
Exception: for maintenance obligations and cooperative debts, up to 50% of the whole salary, minimum wage included, is attachable. The same employee: up to COP 1,500,000
Labour Code art. 149 num. 2
No deduction may reduce pay below the minimum wage or into the non-attachable portion without a court order, even with written employee authorisation
Law 1527 of 2012
Payroll-lending deductions must leave the employee at least 50% of net salary after all statutory deductions
Worked illustration. Employee on COP 3,000,000 with two orders: a maintenance order for COP 1,000,000 per month and a commercial attachment for COP 400,000 per month.
Step
Computation
COP
Gross salary

3,000,000
Mandatory social security (4% + 4%; base below 4 minimum wages so no solidarity fund)
8% × 3,000,000
(240,000)
Withholding tax
Depurated base well below 95 tax units
0
Salary available for garnishment

3,000,000
Maintenance order, art. 156 ceiling
50% × 3,000,000 = 1,500,000 → the order of 1,000,000 fits
(1,000,000)
Commercial attachment, art. 155 ceiling
20% × (3,000,000 − 1,750,905) = 20% × 1,249,095 = 249,819 → the order of 400,000 is capped
(249,819)
Net pay
3,000,000 − 240,000 − 1,000,000 − 249,819
1,510,181
Shortfall on the commercial attachment
400,000 − 249,819 = 150,181
Notified to the issuing court
Note that the two ceilings are tested independently against their own bases, and that the resulting net pay of COP 1,510,181 is below the minimum wage — which is lawful here only because the maintenance order is a court order within the art. 156 exception. A voluntary deduction could not have produced that result.
Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 149, 150, 151, 154, 155, 156; Political Constitution art. 44; Tax Code arts. 823 et seq.; Law 1527 of 2012; Law 1098 of 2006 (Childhood and Adolescence Code).

Accrual methodology.
Vacation accrues continuously from the first day of service at 15 working days per 360 days of
service, i.e. 1.25 working days per month. The monthly employer cost accrual is 15 ÷ 360 = 4.1667% of the vacation base.
Leave with a per-event trigger (sickness, maternity, paternity, bereavement, calamity) does not
accrue; it arises on the event. No balance is carried.
Public holidays and weekly rest are calendar entitlements, not accruals.
Vacation detail — Labour Code arts. 186–192.
Rule
Content
Art. 186 num. 1
"Employees who have rendered their services for one year are entitled to fifteen (15) consecutive working days of paid holiday." Working days, not calendar days
Art. 186 num. 2
15 days per six months for staff in tuberculosis-control establishments and in X-ray work
Art. 187
The employer must set the period no later than the following year, giving 15 days' notice; num. 3 requires a special vacation register recording the entry date, the start and end of each vacation and the remuneration paid
Art. 189 num. 1 (as amended by Law 1429 of 2010 art. 20)
Employer and employee may agree in writing, at the employee's prior request, to pay in money up to half the vacation
Art. 189 num. 3
The base for the cash compensation is the last salary earned
Art. 190
At least 6 continuous working days per year must actually be taken and are non-accumulable; the remainder may be accumulated for up to 2 years, or 4 years for technical, specialised, trusted or management staff and for foreign nationals serving away from their family's residence
Art. 192
During vacation the employee receives "the ordinary salary they are earning on the day the holiday begins"; only rest-day work and supplementary overtime hours are excluded. Variable salary → the average of the immediately preceding year
How are unpaid or partially paid leaves handled?
Unpaid leave (licencia no remunerada) is not a statutory entitlement. It arises by agreement between employer and employee, or as a consequence of a lawful contract suspension under Labour Code art. 51 (force majeure, employer-authorised suspension of activities up to 120 days, the employee's compulsory military service, disciplinary suspension, or a preventive detention).
Aspect
Treatment
Salary
Not payable for the unpaid days. Deduct monthly salary × unpaid days ÷ 30
Transport allowance
Not payable for the unpaid days
Contribution return
Reported with the unpaid-leave change event (SLN). The contribution-return rate table shows health moving to 8.5% or 0% and pension to 12% or 16% depending on the variant, and the payroll levies falling to 0%
Statutory-benefit accrual
Suspended. Labour Code art. 53 provides that during suspension the employee's obligation to work and the employer's obligation to pay are suspended, but the time may be counted for severance-savings and pension purposes at the employer's option and is charged against vacation
Vacation accrual
Suspended for the period of unpaid leave
Seniority
Labour Code art. 53: the periods of suspension may be counted as service for severance savings and pensions, in which case the employer pays the corresponding contributions
Withholding tax
The month's taxable payments are lower; withholding follows automatically
Partially paid leave in Colombia means, in substance, sickness incapacity:

Open item — the rate for the employer's first two days. Decree 2943 of 2013 art. 1, compiled at Decree 780 of 2016 art. 3.2.1.10, allocates *"the cash benefits corresponding to the first two (2)
days of incapacity arising from general illness" to the employer, but does not* state
the rate. Labour Code art. 227 sets the auxilio at two thirds for the first 90 days, and market practice applies 66.67% for days 1–2 on that basis. A minority practice pays 100% for those two days. Mercans default: 66.67%, configurable. Carried in the QA report.
Open item — the minimum-wage floor on incapacity payments. Where 66.67% of the employee's salary falls below the minimum wage, market practice and settled administrative doctrine top the payment up to one minimum wage. The primary regulatory source for that floor was not retrieved this pass. Mercans default: apply the floor, configurable. Carried in the QA report.

The essential contrast: unpaid leave suspends accrual; paid or benefit-covered leave does not.
Are there any mandatory national military service leaves?
Yes. Compulsory military service exists in Colombia for men, under Law 1861 of 2017 (which replaced Law 48 of 1993).
Aspect
Rule
Contract effect
Compulsory military service is a cause of suspension of the employment contract — Labour Code art. 51 num. 4: "the employee being called up to perform military service"
Salary
Not payable during the suspension. This is an unpaid statutory suspension, not paid leave
Reinstatement
Labour Code art. 51 num. 4 obliges the employer to keep the post open and to reinstate the employee on discharge. Law 1861 of 2017 art. 42 reinforces the guarantee
Time to present
The employee must present for reinstatement within 30 days of discharge
Seniority
The period counts for seniority purposes on reinstatement
Social security
Contributions are suspended with the contract; the conscript is covered by the military health system during service
Contribution return
Reported as a suspension change event (SLN) for the affected periods, or as a termination and rehire where the service is long
Reserve call-up / training
Short-term reserve obligations are treated as leave for a compulsory transitory public office under Labour Code art. 57 num. 6 lit. b) — paid
Not a statutory leave in the accrual sense. Military service does not accrue, does not carry a balance and does not generate an employer payment. The engine must model it as a long unpaid suspension with a reinstatement guarantee.
Are any other kinds of leave paid by government authorities?
Yes — this is a defining feature of Colombian leave. The employer pays only a narrow set of short paid absences; the substantial paid leaves are borne by the social-security institutions.

The cash-flow mechanic. Colombian employers customarily advance the benefit through payroll and recover from the health or risk insurer, because the institution pays in arrears and the employee cannot wait. The engine must therefore support a benefit line that is paid to the employee but receivable from the institution, and must not treat it as an employer cost.
Documentation for the paternity benefit is exacting. Labour Code art. 236 par. 2 requires the civil birth registration as the only valid supporting document, to be filed with the health insurer within 30 days of the birth. The benefit is recognised in proportion to the weeks the father contributed during gestation.
What are the payout rules for statutory leaves?
Leave
Payout rule
Vacation
Paid at the ordinary salary being earned on the day the leave begins. Excludes rest-day work and supplementary overtime hours only; the night premium and commissions stay in. Variable salary → the average of the immediately preceding year. The transport allowance is not part of the vacation base — it compensates travel that does not occur during leave
Cash compensation of vacation
Up to half the entitlement, on the employee's prior written request, at the last salary earned (Labour Code art. 189 nums. 1 and 3)
Public holidays and weekly rest
One day's ordinary salary, "but without any deduction being made for absence from work" (art. 177). Where the agreed schedule does not require work on all working days of the week, the rest day is paid in proportion to time worked (art. 173 num. 5)
General-illness incapacity
66.67% for days 1–90; 50% for days 91–180. Variable salary → the average of the year of service preceding the incapacity, or of the whole period if under a year (art. 228)
Occupational incapacity
100% of the contribution base, from the day after the event
Maternity and paternity
Salary earned when the leave begins; variable salary → average of the last year
Bereavement, voting, calamity, medical appointments, summonses
Full ordinary salary; no deduction
A structural point on the paid rest day. Labour Code art. 174 num. 2 provides that "Every salary is understood to include payment for rest on the days on which rest is legally mandatory and paid." For a monthly-salaried employee the paid rest day is already inside the monthly salary and is not a separate payment. For a daily-rated employee it is a distinct payment. The engine must not double-pay the monthly-salaried case.
How are unused paid leaves treated upon termination?
Leave
Treatment on termination
Vacation
Paid out in full, in cash, in proportion to the time worked. This is unconditional — it applies whatever the reason for termination, including dismissal for cause and employee resignation. The 6-day non-accumulable minimum of art. 190 does not bar the payout; it constrains in-service accumulation only
Accumulated vacation from prior years
Paid out in full
Service bonus
Paid pro rata for the semester worked to the termination date
Severance savings
Liquidated and paid to the employee on termination (not deposited to the fund)
Interest on severance savings
Paid to the employee, proportional to the elapsed part of the year
Sickness incapacity in progress
Continues to be paid by the health insurer to the extent of the certified incapacity; a dismissal during certified incapacity carries a separate protection-against-dismissal risk
Public holidays, weekly rest, per-event leaves
No balance; nothing to pay out
Unpaid leave
No balance
Vacation payout formula and worked example.
Vacation payout = base salary (ex overtime and rest-day work)                  x days of service in the accrual period                  / 720
The 720 divisor derives from 15 working days per 360 days of service, expressed in monthly-salary terms: 15 ÷ 30 = 0.5 months of salary per 360 days, so salary × days ÷ 720.
Example. Employee on COP 2,400,000 (no overtime, no rest-day work), terminating on 31 August 2026 with 510 days of service since the last vacation settlement:
2,400,000 × 510 ÷ 720 = COP 1,700,000
Tax on the payout. Vacation compensation is ordinary taxable employment income within the monthly withholding base. It also enters the social-security contribution base and the payroll-levy base, because vacation is paid rest, not a statutory benefit — a point frequently missed on termination settlements.
Provide the calculation logic for each leave type where applicable.
Let S = monthly salary, T = transport allowance (COP 249,095 where due), d = days.
Leave
Formula
Vacation accrual
S_vacation_base × d ÷ 720, where the base excludes overtime and rest-day work
Vacation taken
S_vacation_base × working days of leave ÷ 30
Vacation cash compensation
last salary × days compensated ÷ 720, maximum half the entitlement
Vacation payout on termination
S_vacation_base × days of service ÷ 720
Sickness, days 1–2 (employer)
S ÷ 30 × 0.6667 × d, floored at the minimum wage equivalent
Sickness, days 3–90 (health insurer)
contribution base ÷ 30 × 0.6667 × d, floored at the minimum wage equivalent
Sickness, days 91–180 (health insurer)
contribution base ÷ 30 × 0.50 × d, floored at the minimum wage equivalent
Occupational incapacity
contribution base ÷ 30 × 1.00 × d, from the day after the event
Maternity, 18 weeks = 126 calendar days
S ÷ 30 × 126
Paternity, 2 weeks = 14 calendar days
S ÷ 30 × 14
Bereavement, 5 working days
S ÷ 30 × 5
Public holiday
Already inside a monthly salary (art. 174 num. 2); S ÷ 30 × 1 for a daily-rated employee
Unpaid leave deduction
−(S + T) ÷ 30 × d

Note the two behaviours the example demonstrates: vacation pay is in the contribution base, and contributions continue during incapacity, while the transport allowance is excluded from the base and is not paid for sick days.
How does extended unpaid leave impact social security contributions, tax status, and accrual?
Dimension
Effect of extended unpaid leave
Employment relationship
Suspended, not terminated. Labour Code art. 53: the employee's obligation to work and the employer's obligation to pay are suspended
Health contribution
Reported with the unpaid-leave change event (SLN). The contribution-return rate table shows the health rate moving to 8.5% or 0% depending on the variant. In the 8.5% variant, the employer bears the whole health contribution alone to preserve the employee's health cover
Pension contribution
Rate of 12% or 16% depending on the variant; a public-law employer may contribute 75% of the total rate
Occupational risk
Not payable — there is no exposure while the contract is suspended
Payroll levies
Fall to 0% for the family compensation fund, the apprenticeship training levy and the family welfare levy
Health cover for the employee
Preserved where the employer maintains the health contribution. Extended unpaid leave without contribution risks loss of health cover, and the employer bears the consequence where the suspension was employer-initiated
Tax status
No change to residence or to the withholding procedure. Taxable payments are simply lower or nil for the period. Under Procedure 2 the fixed percentage is unchanged, so a long unpaid absence produces mechanical over-withholding on resumption until the next semi-annual recalculation
Vacation accrual
Suspended
Service-bonus accrual
Suspended — art. 306 accrues on time worked
Severance-savings accrual
Suspended, unless the employer elects under art. 53 to count the period, in which case it pays the corresponding contributions
Seniority for the dismissal indemnity
Follows the same art. 53 election
Return to work
The employee resumes on the same terms. Where the suspension exceeded 120 days for an employer-authorised suspension of activities, the limit of art. 51 num. 3 has been breached and the suspension is unlawful
Contribution-return mechanics
The SLN change event carries the affected day count; the base is reduced pro rata for partial-month suspensions
Configuration rule. Model extended unpaid leave as a suspension with an accrual switch, not as a zero-pay month. The engine must (a) stop the four benefit accruals, (b) report the SLN change event with the correct day count, (c) apply the SLN rate variant the client has elected, and (d) hold the art. 53 seniority election as a per-case flag.
Legal basis: Substantive Labour Code arts. 51, 53, 57 num. 6 and num. 10, 172, 173, 174, 177, 186, 187, 189, 190, 192, 227, 228, 230, 232, 236 (as amended by Law 2114 of 2021 art. 2), 249, 306 (as amended by Law 1788 of 2016 art. 1) and art. 57 as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 15; Law 1280 of 2009 art. 1; Law 1429 of 2010 art. 20; Law 1861 of 2017; Law 2338 of 2023; Law 776 of 2002 art. 3; Decree 2943 of 2013 art. 1 (compiled at Decree 780 of 2016 art. 3.2.1.10); Tax Code art. 206 nums. 1–2 and par. 1; Resolution MSPS 2388 of 2016 Technical Annex 2.

Module 9: Termination & Irregular Payments
Objective: To detail calculation and tax rules for non-standard payments.
9.1 Termination Payments
What are the different types of termination payments?
A Colombian termination settlement (liquidación de prestaciones sociales) is made up of two families of payment: accrued entitlements, which are always due whatever the reason for termination, and indemnities, which are due only in defined circumstances.
Family A — accrued entitlements. Always payable, whatever the reason for termination, including dismissal for cause and employee resignation.
#
Payment
Basis
Legal source

(S + T) × days ÷ 30
Labour Code art. 127
A2
Severance savings (cesantías)
One month's salary per year of service and pro rata for a fraction of a year
Labour Code art. 249
A3

12% per year on the balance, proportional to the elapsed part of the year

Not a termination payment: the employee's own 30-day notice on resignation from an indefinite-term contract (Labour Code art. 47, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 5). That article expressly forbids any penalty on an employee who omits the notice: "In no case may a penalty be agreed for an employee who omits the notice described here."
How do termination rules and calculations differ by residential status and employer type?
By residence status: the labour-law entitlements are identical; only the tax treatment differs.

Temporary employment agency
The agency is the employer of the assigned worker and owes the full settlement. Law 2466 of 2025 art. 45 adds that where the statutory limits on agency use are breached, the user undertaking is treated as the true employer and the agency as a mere intermediary, on a prior judicial declaration
Contractors and subcontractors
Labour Code art. 34, as substituted by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 44 — the principal is jointly and severally liable for the contractor's labour obligations where the work is within the principal's ordinary line of business

Detail the calculation and tax withholding rules for unused leave paid out on termination.
Calculation.
Vacation payout = vacation base × days of service since the last settlement ÷ 720where vacation base = the ordinary salary being earned, EXCLUDING                      rest-day work and supplementary overtime hours only                      (the night premium and commissions stay IN)                      and EXCLUDING the transport allowanceVariable salary     -> average of the immediately preceding year (art. 192 num. 2)
The 720 divisor derives from 15 working days per 360 days of service expressed in monthly-salary terms: 15 ÷ 30 = 0.5 months per 360 days.
Tax withholding.
Aspect
Treatment
Taxable?
Yes, fully. Vacation compensation is ordinary taxable employment income. There is no exemption for it, and it does not carry the art. 206 num. 4 severance-savings exemption
Which base?
The ordinary monthly withholding base of the termination month, under Procedure 1 or Procedure 2
Separate table read?
No. Only the statutory minimum service bonus gets a separate read, and only under Procedure 1
25% exempt-income deduction?
Yes, it participates in the ordinary depuration within the 40% and 790-unit caps
Social security
Contributory. Vacation is paid rest, not a statutory benefit, so the payout enters the contribution base and the payroll-levy base. This is the single most frequently missed line on a Colombian termination settlement
Non-resident
Flat 20% on the gross under art. 408
Describe the rules for statutory severance/redundancy payments.
Colombia has no redundancy regime as such. There is no economic-dismissal procedure, no collective redundancy consultation for the ordinary case, and no separate redundancy payment. A dismissal for economic reasons is simply a dismissal without just cause, compensated by the art. 64 indemnity.
What Colombia does have instead is severance *savings* — a different instrument entirely.
(a) Severance savings (*auxilio de cesantía*) — Labour Code art. 249.
"Every employer is obliged to pay its employees … on termination of the employment contract, as severance savings (auxilio de cesantía), one month of salary for each year of service and proportionally for any fraction of a year."
Element
Rule
Entitlement
One month's salary per year of service, pro rata for a fraction
Base
Labour Code art. 253: the last monthly salary, provided it has not varied in the last 3 months; otherwise, and for variable salaries, the average of the last year of service, or of the whole period if shorter. The base includes the transport allowance
Annual regime (Law 50 of 1990 art. 99, for all employees hired from 1 January 1991)
Liquidated at 31 December each year and deposited to the employee's chosen fund before 15 February (i.e. by 14 February). Late deposit penalty: one day's salary for each day of delay
Monthly advance deposit — new
Law 2466 of 2025 art. 64 adds a paragraph to Law 50 of 1990 art. 99 permitting the employer to deposit 8.33% of the base monthly as an advance deposit. This is not a partial payment of severance savings — the employee can access the money only in the cases the law allows or on termination
Monthly interest — new
The same article permits the parties to agree in writing to pay the interest monthly, at 1% of the base
Direct payment to the employee
Labour Code art. 57 par. 4, added by Law 2466 of 2025 art. 15: where the employer proves direct payment to the employee, that payment is discharging and no non-deposit penalty applies, unless it is shown that the payment was not made freely or was not applied to a legally authorised purpose. Affiliation to a severance-savings fund nonetheless remains compulsory
Partial payments
Labour Code art. 254 prohibits partial payment before termination except in the expressly authorised cases (housing acquisition, improvement or debt release; education of the employee, spouse or children), on pain of losing the sums paid
Loss of the right — art. 250
Delictual acts against the employer or the employer's relatives; intentional grave material damage to buildings, works, machinery or raw materials; disclosure of technical or commercial secrets causing grave prejudice. The employer may withhold payment until a court decides
Pre-1991 regime
Employees hired before 1 January 1991 who did not migrate remain on the retrospective annual-adjustment regime. Reported as a separate line on the information return
Construction workers — art. 310
Special regime: 3 days' salary per completed month
All-inclusive salary
Absorbed
(b) Interest on severance savings — Law 52 of 1975 art. 1.
"… shall recognise and pay interest of 12% per annum on the balances that, at 31 December of each year, or on the dates of the employee's departure or of a partial settlement of severance savings, the employee holds to their credit in respect of severance savings. … The interest … must be paid in the month of January of the following year … or on the date of the employee's departure … in an amount proportional to the part of the year elapsed. … If the employer fails to pay the employee the interest established here … it must pay the wage earner, by way of indemnity and on one occasion only, the amount [equal to the interest owed]."
The interest is paid to the employee directly, not to the fund — unlike the principal.
(c) Indemnity for dismissal without just cause — Labour Code art. 64.
Employee's salary
≤ 1 year of service
Each subsequent year and pro rata
Below 10 minimum wages (< COP 17,509,050)
30 days' salary
+20 days' salary
10 minimum wages or above
20 days' salary
+15 days' salary
Transitional paragraph: employees with 10 or more years of continuous service at the entry into force of Law 789 of 2002 retain the earlier Law 50 of 1990 art. 6 literals b), c) and d) scale.
(d) The social-security clearance condition — Labour Code art. 65 par. 1. An easily missed gate:
"In order to proceed with the termination of the employment contract …, the employer must inform the employee in writing, at the last registered address, within the sixty (60) days following the termination, of the payment status of the social security and payroll-levy contributions on the salaries of the last three months preceding the termination, attaching the payment receipts … If the employer does not prove payment of those contributions, the termination of the contract shall have no effect. However, the employer may pay the contributions during the following sixty (60) days, with default interest."
(e) Statement of cause — Labour Code art. 66. The terminating party must state the cause at the moment of termination; different causes may not validly be alleged afterwards.
Provide the statutory formula for any tax-free portion.
The tax-free portion attaches to the severance savings and their interest, and to nothing else.
Tax Code art. 206 num. 4:

Tax Code art. 206 nums. 1–3 exempt accident, illness and maternity-protection indemnities, and art. 206 par. 1 limits even those to the statutory minimum amount: "operates only on the amounts corresponding to the statutory minimum … the excess is not exempt". A dismissal indemnity under art. 64 is not within nums. 1–3 and is fully taxable

None
Also excluded from the base entirely. Severance savings and their interest are excluded from the monthly withholding base under both Procedure 1 and Procedure 2 (Tax Code arts. 385 and 386). They are not simply exempt — they never enter the monthly calculation at all, and the art. 206 num. 4 exemption is applied when the payment itself is made. Also, the 25% exemption of art. 206 num. 10 does not apply to severance savings (art. 206 par. 2).

Deliverable: Detail the rules for any applicable concessional tax cap
Not Applicable — Colombia has no concessional tax cap on termination payments.
There is no equivalent of a lifetime or per-event cap on concessionally taxed termination payments, no tax-free redundancy allowance and no reduced rate for termination income. The only concessional treatment in the Colombian termination settlement is the art. 206 num. 4 exemption on severance savings and their interest, which is:
income-tested, not capped by amount — the test is the average monthly income of the last six months
of employment, and the exemption tapers from 100% to 0% across the 350-to-650-tax-unit range;
not a lifetime allowance — it applies to each termination on its own facts;
not extendable to any other termination payment.
Three related caps exist and must not be confused with a concessional termination cap:
Cap
What it limits
Applies to termination?
790 tax units per year on the 25% exempt income (art. 206 num. 10)
The general labour-income exemption
Yes, to the ordinary monthly base of the termination month — but not to severance savings (art. 206 par. 2)
1,340 tax units per year on combined deductions and exempt income (art. 336 num. 3)
The total of deductions and exemptions
Yes, to the ordinary base
40% of the post-non-taxable-income figure (art. 388)
The same total
Yes, to the ordinary base
Also not applicable: there is no averaging, no spreading, no top-slicing and no separate termination schedule. The dismissal indemnity is taxed as ordinary employment income in the month it is paid.

9.2 Irregular and Retrospective Payments
Describe the specific tax calculation method required for irregular payments.
Colombia has no special method for irregular payments. There is no averaging, no spreading, no top-slicing, no annualisation and no separate schedule. An irregular payment is added to the taxable payments of the month in which it is paid, and the ordinary calculation runs.
The four exceptions, all of which are rules about particular payments, not about irregularity as such:
Exception
Rule
Statutory minimum service bonus, Procedure 1
Separate read of the art. 383 table on the bonus alone (art. 385)
Severance savings and interest
Excluded from the monthly base under both procedures; art. 206 num. 4 exemption and taper applied on payment
Procedure 2 generally
The fixed percentage smooths irregular payments across the semester by construction — the payment raises the base the percentage is applied to but does not change the percentage until the next June or December recalculation
Sub-30-day periods, Procedure 1
Gross to 30 days, read the table percentage, apply that percentage to the actual payment (art. 385)
The consequence, stated plainly. Under Procedure 1 a large irregular payment in a single month pushes the depurated base into a higher marginal band for that month only, producing a spike in withholding. That is arithmetically correct — the employee did receive that income in that month — and it is reconciled in the employee's own annual return, not by the employer. Under Procedure 2 the same payment produces a smaller spike, because the fixed percentage is unchanged.
Two caps bite hardest on irregular payments, and the engine must apply them monthly:
Cap
Monthly figure 2026
790 tax units per year on the 25% exempt income
65.8333 units = COP 3,447,955
1,340 tax units per year on combined deductions and exempt income
111.6667 units = COP 5,848,430
A one-off payment large enough to make 25% of the depurated base exceed COP 3,447,955 will hit the first cap — as demonstrated in the retention-bonus example in Module 6.3 and the termination example above.
Social security on irregular payments. An irregular payment that is salary enters the contribution base of the month it is paid, subject to the 25-minimum-wage ceiling and, where a non-salary pact applies, to the 40% clamp. A large one-off payment is therefore commonly the month in which the ceiling or the clamp bites for the first time.
How are backdated payments and retrospective salary adjustments handled?
The controlling principle is that Colombian payroll is settled on a *payment* basis, not an accrual basis, for tax and social security alike. A retrospective payment is generally taxed and contributed in the month it is paid, not in the months to which it relates.
Aspect
Treatment
Withholding tax
The arrears are added to the taxable payments of the month of payment and the ordinary calculation runs. There is no re-opening of prior months and no re-computation of prior withholding. Tax Code art. 383 applies to "payments or credits to account" — payments or credits — so the trigger is the payment
Statutory benefits
The arrears retrospectively increase the base for the service bonus, severance savings, interest and vacation for the periods to which they relate. These must be recalculated and topped up, because the statutory benefit is measured on the salary that was actually due
Overtime and premiums
A retrospective salary increase raises the ordinary hour value for the affected periods, so overtime and premiums already paid must be topped up
Electronic payroll XML
Reported through an adjustment note (NominaIndividualDeAjuste) where a validated document must be corrected, or within the current month's ordinary document where the arrears are simply a payment of the current month
Form 350
The arrears withholding is declared in the month of payment. No prior return is corrected
Social security is the exception that requires a prior-period correction. Contributions follow the accrual of the salary, because the contribution base is defined by the salary earned in the period. Where a retrospective increase raises the base of past months, the employer must file a correction planilla (type N) for those months, reporting paired records — an A group with the originally settled values and a C group with the corrected values — and pay the additional contributions with late-payment interest. The sum of the contribution bases and of the day counts in the C records must be greater than or equal to those in the A records, which is precisely the under-declaration case a retrospective increase produces. The correction may be filed only through the same information operator that carried the original return.
Practical sequence for a retrospective salary increase.
1. Compute the arrears of BASIC SALARY for each affected month.2. Recompute the ORDINARY HOUR VALUE for each affected month and top up   overtime, the night premium and the rest-day premium.   -> remember the 2026 divisor split: 220 to 14 July, 210 from 15 July.3. Recompute the STATUTORY BENEFIT bases and top up:      service bonus, severance savings, interest, vacation.4. Pay the whole arrears amount in the CURRENT month.5. TAX: add the arrears to the current month's taxable payments; run the   ordinary calculation; declare on the current month's Form 350.6. SOCIAL SECURITY: file a CORRECTION PLANILLA for each affected month,   with paired A/C records, and pay the additional contributions with   late-payment interest.7. Report the arrears in the current month's electronic payroll document,   or by adjustment note where a validated document must be corrected.
Two 2026-specific retrospective triggers. Both arise from mid-year statutory changes and both produce genuine arrears if not applied on time:
The rest-day premium step from 80% to 90% on 1 July 2026. An employer that continued at 80% into
July owes arrears on every rest-day hour worked from 1 July.
The hourly divisor change from 220 to 210 on 15 July 2026. An employer that continued at 220 owes
arrears of approximately 4.8% on every premium hour from 15 July.
For arrears, how are contributions and deductions computed?
Item
Computation
Employee health 4%
On the corrected contribution base of each affected month, not on the payment month. File a correction planilla and recover the employee's share from the arrears payment
Employee pension 4%
Same
Solidarity pension fund
Same. Note that arrears can retrospectively trigger the 4-minimum-wage threshold for a month in which it was not previously triggered, or move the employee into a higher subsistence band
Employer pension 12%, health 8.5%, occupational risk
On the corrected base of each affected month, with late-payment interest
Payroll levies 4% / 2% / 3%
On the corrected payroll-levy base of each affected month
The exemption test
Re-tested on the corrected month's earnings. Arrears can retrospectively push a month above 10 minimum wages, retrospectively losing the exemption for that month and creating a liability for employer health, the apprenticeship levy and the family welfare levy on the whole of that month's base
Contribution-base ceiling
Re-tested per affected month at 25 minimum wages. Arrears that would take a past month above the ceiling are capped for that month
The 40% clamp
Re-tested per affected month
Withholding tax
On the payment month, not the affected months
Voluntary deductions
Not recomputed retrospectively; applied in the payment month, subject to the minimum-wage floor
Late-payment interest
Payable on the additional contributions, computed from each original due date
The retrospective exemption trap deserves emphasis. Because the Tax Code art. 114-1 test is "menos de diez (10) salarios mínimos" applied per employee, per month, on what the employee earns in that month, a retrospective increase that takes a past month's earnings from COP 17,000,000 to COP 17,600,000 loses the exemption for that entire month — creating a retrospective liability of 8.5% + 2% + 3% on the whole corrected base, roughly COP 2.4 million for that single month. An engine that applies arrears only prospectively will not detect this.

The comparison makes the Colombian structuring rule concrete: a non-salary pact is a social-security and statutory-benefit optimisation, it does not reduce tax — indeed it slightly increases withholding, because the lower contributions mean less non-taxable income — and it is clamped at 40%.
